# todo.md — Universal Task File

> **This is the ONLY task file in fasterRag. No other todo/task/tracking file may ever be created. All tasks live here.**
>
> Rules:
> - Open task: `- [ ] TASK-0001: <description>`
> - Completed task: `- [x] TASK-0001: <description> — ✅ YYYY-MM-DD`
> - **Append-only rule:** when a task is completed, tick the checkbox and append the completion date in `YYYY-MM-DD` format. After ticking, that entry is append-only and must NOT be edited further.
> - Task IDs (`TASK-`/`AUDIT-`) are sequential and never reused. Audit-gap tasks use the `AUDIT-` prefix.
> - [blockers.md](blockers.md) is a **read-only view** of the entries below that are waiting on a human — it holds no task of its own and every line there cites an id from this file. It is not an exception to the rule above; it is a table of contents for one slice of it.

## Pending

- [ ] TASK-0020: Decide beta version number and stamp CHANGELOG Unreleased → 0.1.0-beta.1 on first release — **decided 2026-09-13, stamp deferred to release step 3 by design.** The number is `0.1.0-beta.1` (tag `v0.1.0-beta.1`, PEP 440 `0.1.0b1`), recorded in release.md's checklist with the reasoning: `0.1.0` because the surface is complete enough to use and not stable enough to promise, `-beta` rather than `-alpha` because what ships is tested rather than exploratory, numbered so `-beta.2` follows without burning a minor. **The stamp itself is deliberately not done now** — a beta label on an unfrozen tree names builds that are not the beta, and a dated CHANGELOG heading for a release that has not happened is a false record; the ledger line always said *on first release*. What landed instead is `scripts/stamp_release.py`: one command performs the four mechanical edits that step 3 previously asked a human to make under release-day pressure (`__version__`, the CHANGELOG heading, the Trove development-status classifier, and a preserved empty Unreleased section), and `--check` is a **CI gate holding the tree to one of two coherent states** — wholly development or wholly released. A wheel whose `__version__` says beta while its own README says "not yet on PyPI" is drift a reader cannot tell from a lie, and that half-stamped state is now uncommittable. The three prose banners (TASK-0256) are reported, never rewritten: the gate names each document still promising nothing is published, and a human deletes them. 22 unit tests including both half-done directions, a CRLF/LF preservation property, and a dry run against copies of the real files (three surgical diffs, no line-ending churn). Ticks when the stamp is made

GPL-3.0-or-later had been declared from the first commit with nothing recording it as a choice, and its copyleft contradicted the stated adoption thesis of teams embedding fasterRag in closed-source products. Apache-2.0 over MIT for the explicit patent grant; no dependency constrained the choice. Applied in one change so no two artifacts disagree: the canonical text replaces `LICENSE`, `NOTICE` carries the copyright line and ships in the sdist, and `pyproject.toml` declares `Apache-2.0` in both the `license` field and the trove classifier — the wheel's METADATA was built and inspected to confirm it says Apache and not GPL. Blocker B1 retired; its PyPI child moved under B2, which is now the last irreversible item before the first upload)

### S3 — Ingestion core

- [x] TASK-0030: Implement contextual enrichment (P2) — `core/chunking/enrichment.py` prepends a document-level context to each chunk before embedding and indexing. The parent document leads every prompt verbatim so it stays a cacheable prefix; without that, cost scales with document length times chunk count rather than chunk count alone. Calls run concurrently, since one round trip per chunk in sequence would make ingestion latency the sum of every chunk's call. **A failed enrichment is never fatal** — the chunk indexes unprefixed and carries `enrichment_failed`, because a slightly worse chunk beats a dead-lettered document, and an unflagged failure would be indistinguishable from the feature being off. `chunking.context_tokens` is interpolated into the prompt rather than hardcoded; a setting the prompt ignores silently does nothing. **Verified against the live provider** on a policies fixture: the model returned the section, region, year, and the referents the chunk's shorthand depends on, with `original_text` preserved and zero failures. 16 tests — ✅ 2026-08-04
- [x] TASK-0185: Wire enrichment into ingestion — `chunking.contextual_enrichment: true` now changes what is indexed. The process-boundary constraint resolved as documented: parsing stays in the process pool, the parsed text comes back on `ParseOutcome.document_text`, and the provider call happens on the async side after the executor returns — so parsing stays parallel and CPU-bound while an I/O wait sits on the event loop where it belongs. The parent text is carried **only when the toggle is on**, or every parsed document would double what it sends through IPC for a field nothing reads. The enrichment model is built only when the toggle is on, and a missing model leaves the flag inert rather than raising — a toggle cannot conjure a provider. **Verified against the live provider through the real pool**: two documents in, two enriched, originals preserved. 4 wiring tests on top of the 16 unit tests. `fasterrag estimate` does not yet account for the extra calls — TASK-0186 — ✅ 2026-08-04
- [x] TASK-0186: `fasterrag estimate` accounts for contextual-enrichment calls — reported as its own block rather than blended into the embedding figure, because enrichment is a generation charge on a different model at different rates and one combined number hides which knob to turn. The prompt cost is document tokens × chunk count, which is the term that makes it expensive and the term a per-chunk figure would conceal. Quoted **uncached** and the basis string says so: prompt caching is what makes enrichment affordable, but its discount depends on the provider and cache window, so a number fasterRag cannot verify would understate a real bill. An unpriced model reports `unknown`, never zero — zero reads as free. Verified on the policies corpus: 12 calls, $0.0011, against $0.0000 for local embedding. 8 tests, including one asserting the estimator loads no tokenizer — a preflight that pulls a multi-gigabyte model is not a cheap preflight — ✅ 2026-08-04
- [x] TASK-0113: Implement the pooling half of late chunking in the embedding pool (embed the document in one long-context pass, then pool token representations over each chunk's span); the boundary half and the `late_pooling` marker already ship — ✅ 2026-08-05 (`core/chunking/pooling.py` runs the model over the whole document and mean-pools the token states inside each chunk's span. Verified against the real local `BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5`: on a three-sentence document whose last chunk reads "It was raised from 35 pounds", the query "what was the UK meal allowance raised from" retrieves the *wrong* chunk with ordinary embedding and the right one with pooling — an illustration of the failure mode, not a benchmark entry. Documents beyond the model's context are processed in overlapping windows, each token taking its state from the window that gave it the most surrounding context, so a 1,440-token document on a 512-token model pools correctly instead of being truncated. A hosted provider cannot supply token-level output, so the pool falls back to ordinary embedding rather than failing — `late` is then no better than `recursive`, not broken. The document text reaches the embedding stage as one shared string object attached on the parent side, so it costs a pointer per chunk rather than a copy: measured 1 distinct object across a document's chunks. **A corpus-corrupting hazard was found while wiring it** — pooling regroups a batch by document, so writing the original batch order against pooled vectors pairs every chunk with another chunk's vector, producing a corpus that indexes cleanly and retrieves nonsense; the write now follows the regrouped order and a test fails if that is reverted)
- [x] TASK-0114: Replace the estimating token counter with the embedding provider's real tokenizer once an embedding adapter is configured, so `chunking.chunk_size` counts true tokens rather than a four-characters-per-token estimate — ✅ 2026-08-04 (measured against the local `BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5` tokenizer: the estimate undercounted Japanese by 4.3× and code by 1.9×, so a `chunk_size: 256` chunk of Japanese was really 1,092 tokens and was silently truncated at the model's 512 limit. Two defects were found while wiring it: the counter was only reporting `token_count` and never constrained the split, and `overlap` was applied purely in characters. Both now enforce a real token budget — every chunk in the three-corpus check fits the model limit, and English prose chunks byte-identically to before. Exposed as `chunking.token_counter` and `chunking.chars_per_token`)
- [x] TASK-0188: Expand directory arguments so `fasterrag ingest <dir>` indexes the files inside it — ✅ 2026-08-05 (found while verifying `--set` end to end: `--recursive` was declared in the parser and read nowhere, and a directory source became one unreadable document, so `ingest ./corpus` reported "0 chunks from 0 documents" and exited 0. The most obvious way to use the CLI silently indexed nothing. Expansion happens at the CLI boundary rather than in the service, so the API is not handed a server-side directory walk; hidden files and `.git`/`node_modules`/virtualenvs are skipped, and files are sorted because document ids derive from job order)
- [x] TASK-0187: Add a global `--set dotted.key=value` so any setting can be overridden per invocation — ✅ 2026-08-05 (merged into the raw mapping *before* validation, so an override is held to the same range, type, unknown-key, and cross-field rules a file value is — assigning onto a built `Settings` would have skipped exactly the checks that catch a bad combination. Every command routes through one `settings_from(args)` helper, because a command calling `load_settings` directly would accept the flag and silently ignore it)
- [x] TASK-0186: Refuse `embeddings.dimensions` when a local model cannot produce that width — ✅ 2026-08-05 (reproduced: `dimensions: 256` with `BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5` made the adapter declare 256 while emitting 384, so the collection was created at the wrong width and every upsert failed later with nothing naming the setting. Now refused at load time with both sizes in the message. Truncation is deliberately not offered — it is only lossless for a Matryoshka-trained model, and hosted providers that support it already do it server-side)
- [x] TASK-0185: Expose the retrieval, chunking, and cache tuning constants that were hardcoded — ✅ 2026-08-04 (`retrieval.bm25_k1`/`bm25_b`, `chunking.semantic_percentile`, `cache.max_entries`, `embeddings.cache.max_entries`. Each has a test asserting the value reaches its consumer, because a setting that validates but is never read reads as a supported control and silently does nothing. The BM25 pair is documented as requiring a reindex: it changes the stored sparse vectors, not just query-time scoring)
- [x] TASK-0218: Expose the parsing constants TASK-0185 left hardcoded — ✅ 2026-08-09 (a new `parsing` section: `minimum_chars_per_page` (the OCR trigger), `ocr_resolution`, `heading_size_ratio`, `max_heading_chars`, `rows_per_block`. The first two are why this exists — a corpus of scans needs a different trigger and a different render than a born-digital one, and tuning either meant editing `core/parsing`. Configuration reaches the parsers as a frozen `ParsingOptions` value object built by `create_parsing_options(settings)`, the same shape `create_chunker` uses: the factory reads the schema at the boundary and the component never sees `Settings`, which is what keeps `fasterrag.parsing` usable standalone with no `config.yaml` at all. The options are derived *inside* the worker from settings that already cross the process boundary, so nothing extra is pickled per document. Dispatch splits into two tables — the five formats with nothing to tune keep their two-argument signature rather than carrying a dead parameter that implies a knob they do not have. Every setting has a test asserting it changes what the parser does, plus one asserting the values survive the settings → worker → parser path, plus one asserting `ParsingOptions()` still equals the schema defaults so no behaviour moved. **Deliberately left hardcoded**: `_MAX_HEADING_LEVEL` (6 is the format's own ceiling in Markdown, DOCX, and HTML, not a preference), `_SNIFF_BYTES` (how much of a CSV the dialect sniffer reads — an implementation detail of dialect detection with no corpus-level meaning), and the HTML dropped-element and content-element sets (format semantics; a configurable "is `<nav>` content" is a parser rewrite, not a knob). One consequence documented rather than fixed: parsing settings are not part of `chunker_config_hash`, so changing one leaves chunk ids and the content hash untouched and a re-ingest of an unchanged corpus is deduplicated — config-reference.md says so and points at `index reembed`; folding them into the hash would renumber every chunk in every existing collection)
- [x] TASK-0184: Add `fasterrag config show` so every configurable setting, its effective value, and its default are discoverable from the CLI — ✅ 2026-08-04 (walks the schema rather than a hand-written list, so a new field needs no registration; `--changed` lists only overrides. Loads with `require_env=False` because it is most useful on the installation `config validate` refuses)
- [ ] TASK-0115: (maintainer decision) `sentence-transformers` ships as the `huggingface` extra rather than in the core install, because it pulls a multi-gigabyte deep-learning runtime that a hosted-provider deployment should not have to download. `docs/python-api.md` was updated to match. Reverting to core is a one-line change in `pyproject.toml` if the original packaging promise should stand
- [x] TASK-0119: Rebuilding a collection is the only way to add a keyword leg — Qdrant cannot attach a sparse vector to an existing dense-only collection, and the two layouts (unnamed dense versus named dense plus named sparse) are not interchangeable. Flipping `retrieval.hybrid` on an indexed corpus therefore needs the D2 blue/green reindex path, and until that exists the adapter refuses the mismatch rather than dropping the sparse vector silently. **Detection landed 2026-11-04**: `services/reindex.audit_keyword_leg` reports whether a live collection carries the leg its configuration asks for, and `require_keyword_leg` raises on the mismatch, so the condition is now nameable before an ingest run dies at its first upsert. 7 tests. **Wired 2026-11-07**: `POST /v1/ingest` and `POST /v1/ingest/{job_id}/retry-dlq` call `require_keyword_leg` **before a job record exists**, so a misconfigured run is refused with a 409 `CONFLICT` naming the collection, the rebuild command and the opt-out — instead of being accepted with a `202` and then dying in a background task whose detail reaches only the server log, under the actively misleading code `EMBED_PROVIDER_ERROR` ("Embedding provider failed", 503) when the embedding provider is fine. **Acceptance is the only place the caller is still listening**: `GET /v1/ingest/{job_id}` returns `{"status": "failed", "counts": {...}}` and nothing else, so refusing early is the difference between a message and no message, not merely an earlier one. `fasterrag index create` re-checks *after* creating, which is the only way the CLI can tell that a backend accepted a create whose keyword leg it never added. Deliberately on the write path and not the query path: one metadata round trip per job is noise beside a corpus that takes minutes to hours, where the same probe per query would charge every question for a mistake made once and report it to a reader who cannot act on it. Startup was rejected too — the API boots with an unreachable backend and serves degraded by design, so a startup refusal would turn a transient outage into a failure to boot. 10 tests (8 API, 2 CLI), including one asserting the old confusing failure no longer reaches the operator. **It catches Qdrant independently of TASK-0305**, because the audit reads live server state through `CollectionInfo.sparse` rather than trusting what `create_collection` was asked for — a collection Qdrant waved through still reads `sparse=False` and is still refused. **Fakes only**: no Docker daemon on the build machine, so nothing here has run against a live Qdrant, pgvector or Chroma, and the "before" behaviour is traced through the code rather than reproduced. Three write paths remain ungated (TASK-0320) — ✅ 2026-11-07
- [x] TASK-0116: Expose the per-document status endpoints over REST (`GET /v1/ingest/{job_id}`, `GET /v1/ingest/{job_id}/documents?status=…`, `POST /v1/ingest/{job_id}/retry-dlq`); the journal already answers these queries — ✅ 2026-08-01
- [x] TASK-0128: Implement the ingest, query, retrieve, collections, and admin routers, completing the REST half of the control plane — ✅ 2026-08-01
- [x] TASK-0129: `POST /v1/ingest` accepts only `path` sources; `url` and `inline` are documented in api-reference.md but rejected with `VALIDATION_FAILED` naming what is supported, because no loader fetches them yet. Ships with the source loader — shipped as `services/sources.py`: urls are fetched with the size limit enforced both on the declared length and while streaming (a header a peer controls is not a limit), inline payloads decode base64-first with UTF-8 fallback, and both stage to a per-job temp directory that is removed when the job settles. The canonical URI — not the temp path — remains the document's identity, so re-ingesting the same url or snippet still dedups; a bare string with an http(s) scheme classifies as a url, so `fasterrag ingest <url>` works as the CLI documents; `httpx` moved from the dev extra to core dependencies since the loader imports it at runtime — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [ ] TASK-0130: Ingestion runs as an in-process background task rather than through a queue backend. The documented contract (`202` + job id, poll `GET /v1/ingest/{job_id}`) is satisfied identically by a distributed queue, so moving to one changes no response — but a restart mid-job currently relies on journal resume rather than queue redelivery. Maintainer decision on the queue backend

### S5 — Rerank + eval harness v1

- [x] TASK-0077: Implement the golden-set generator (P4) — stratified sampling, adversarial records, `source: autopilot` provenance; verified against a real 21-chunk PDF corpus producing 10/10 records with 2 adversarial and 0 dropped — ✅ 2026-08-01
- [x] TASK-0139: Commit a golden set and a measured baseline for a fixture corpus under `tests/eval/datasets/` — the `handbook` fixture: 6 documents, 15 hand-authored records (12 answerable, 3 adversarial), document-level ground truth, and a baseline measured against a real Qdrant at recall@5 1.0 / MRR 1.0 / nDCG@5 1.0 — ✅ 2026-08-01
- [x] TASK-0141: On Windows a document id hashes the source path string, so the same file ingested as `d:\...` and `D:\...` gets two different document ids. Content-hash dedup still prevents a double index, but `index.lock` document hashes and drift detection would treat them as different documents. Found while building the eval baseline, where a resolved-vs-globbed path produced a recall of 0.0 that looked like a retrieval failure. Consider normalising the source URI before hashing, platform-aware — `normalise_source` folds Windows paths (drive letter, separator, and case) before hashing, so `d:\docs.md`, `D:\docs.md`, and `D:/docs/a.md` are one document. URLs are left untouched: their paths are case-sensitive by specification, and folding one would merge two genuinely different resources. POSIX paths keep their case for the same reason — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0142: The handbook baseline detects large regressions but not small ones. Verified to catch an index missing four of six documents (recall 1.0 → 0.3333, gate blocked, exit 5), but at six documents and k=5 a retriever returns five of six, so a *subtle* ranking regression would pass. A discriminating baseline needs a larger fixture with topically overlapping documents — added `tests/eval/datasets/policies`: 12 documents in three families of four near-identical siblings (region x year, and role), so each query's answer must beat three look-alikes rather than five unrelated documents. Measured baseline **recall@5 1.0, MRR 0.875, nDCG@5 0.9077** — recall saturates while ranking keeps headroom, which is exactly what the handbook fixture lacks. Proven against a real regression: disabling the BM25 leg moves the new fixture to MRR 0.8125 / nDCG 0.8616 (−0.046, past the 0.02 tolerance) while the handbook fixture stays at a flat 1.0/1.0 and sees nothing — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0143: Dropping a collection without clearing the journal makes a re-ingest a correct no-op — the content hashes are remembered per collection, so nothing is re-indexed and the collection is never recreated. That is D3 behaving as designed, but it is a sharp edge during recovery; `disaster-recovery.md` §4 documents restore-from-snapshot as the path, and should say explicitly that re-ingest is not an alternative unless the journal is cleared too — `disaster-recovery.md` §4 now carries an **Index lost, journal intact** row stating that re-ingest is not a recovery path, plus a "re-ingest trap" subsection explaining why deduplication cannot distinguish "already indexed" from "indexed into an index that no longer exists", what the `deduplicated` count looks like when it happens, and the two supported ways out — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0140: Expose golden-set generation on the CLI (`fasterrag autopilot generate-golden-set` or similar). Today it is a library call only, so the shared machinery Autopilot (D6) depends on is reachable from Python but not from the terminal — `fasterrag autopilot generate-golden-set <sources...>` parses and chunks the corpus exactly as ingestion would, so no collection is needed and none is read; a golden set is usually wanted before the index exists. Refuses to overwrite an existing file, since a generated set is hand-curated afterwards. Verified live against the handbook corpus: 6 records, 1 adversarial, 0 dropped, with sensible questions — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0120: Add the faithfulness metric to the eval harness once the generation slice provides the P3 call site; the harness reports retrieval metrics only until then — `score_faithfulness` answers every answerable golden query and grades it with P3, reporting the mean plus counts of refused and ungraded. Kept as a separate entry point, never called by retrieval scoring: a hundred-record set is two hundred provider calls, and a metrics run that silently started generating would bill for itself. Adversarial records are excluded — they are deliberately unanswerable, so grading one would score the system down for correctly refusing — and refusals are counted rather than folded in as zeros — ✅ 2026-08-03

### S6 — Generation

- [x] TASK-0037: Implement generation service with SSE streaming (meta/token/citations/usage/done/error events) — ✅ 2026-07-31
- [x] TASK-0078: Implement grounded-or-refuse mode with faithfulness scoring and INSUFFICIENT_EVIDENCE responses (D5) — ✅ 2026-07-31
- [ ] TASK-0123: Maintainer review — no doc specified how grounded-or-refuse behaves while streaming, and refusing after tokens have been sent is impossible. Implemented as: with `generation.grounded_or_refuse: true` the stream buffers the answer, grades it, then emits either the whole answer or one `insufficient_evidence` event followed by `done`. This trades time-to-first-token for the guarantee, only when the flag is on. Recorded in `docs/api-reference.md` §Streaming semantics; confirm or choose the alternative (stream tokens and emit a retraction event)

### S7 — Semantic cache

- [x] TASK-0038: Implement embedding cache + semantic response cache (cosine threshold, TTL + corpus-change invalidation, hit/miss metrics) — ✅ 2026-07-31
- [x] TASK-0124: Implement the `redis` cache backend behind a `.[redis]` extra. `embeddings.cache.backend` and `cache.backend` both accept `redis` per config-reference.md, but only `memory` and `disk` are implemented; selecting `redis` raises a `ConfigError` naming the alternatives rather than silently falling back. Needs a Docker Redis for the integration test — `RedisStore` ships in `core/cache/redis.py`, wired through `_create` and reached by `embeddings.cache.redis_url` / `cache.redis_url` (new settings, both defaulting to `redis://localhost:6379/0`). The `ConfigError` is replaced, not bypassed: it now fires only when the optional client is absent, naming `pip install fasterrag[redis]`, and the client is imported lazily inside the constructor so nothing else pays for the dependency. **The entry ceiling is enforced by a sorted-set index rather than left to Redis**, because `maxmemory-policy` is server-wide, needs server configuration we do not own, and would evict a co-tenant's keys as readily as ours — while counting our own by scanning the keyspace is O(everything stored). Reads restack the score, so eviction is least-recently-*used*, matching `MemoryStore`. The index is also what `items` walks and what `clear` deletes: `clear` is never `FLUSHDB`, so a corpus change cannot wipe a neighbour's data, and the two caches take separate namespaces (`fasterrag:embedding`, `fasterrag:semantic`) so one server can back both. Index is written before the value on purpose — interrupted the other way round it would leave a value no index knows about, invisible to the ceiling, to `items`, and to `clear`, which is a leak rather than a miss. TTL is written twice, into the payload (authoritative, so an expired entry is a miss on every backend) and as the key's native expiry (so Redis reclaims the memory). `decode_responses` is forced off: values are packed float32 behind a struct header, and a decoding client turns every one into a permanent miss. Every failure is translated to `CacheError` at the boundary and the message never echoes the URL, which can carry a password. 36 unit tests against a fake client plus 9 integration tests against a real `redis:7` container on port 6399 (a distinct name and port, so a developer's own Redis is never adopted or removed) — ✅ 2026-08-09

- [x] TASK-0125: The error-code table has no code for "the vector database rejected our credentials". The Qdrant adapter currently reports `EMBED_PROVIDER_ERROR`, documented as "Embedding provider timeout / hard failure", so a vector-DB auth failure surfaces under an embedding label — misleading when grepping logs. `AUTH_INVALID` is reserved for fasterRag's own API auth and `RETRIEVAL_FAILED` is documented retryable, which this is not. Add a `VECTOR_DB_AUTH` code to `errors.py` and `api-reference.md`, then use it in `_auth_error`. Found by running `fasterrag index list` against a key-protected Qdrant, 2026-07-31 — added `VECTOR_DB_AUTH_FAILED` (503, non-retryable). Sending an operator to check their embedding provider over a rejected Qdrant key is a wrong answer with a confident tone; the taxonomy exists to point at the right knob — ✅ 2026-08-03

### S8 — CLI complete

- [x] TASK-0040: Implement CLI (serve, worker, ingest, query, index, provision, status, doctor, estimate, config validate) — `replay`, `benchmark`, `export`, `import`, and `autopilot` are registered but report the slice that ships them, since their services do not exist yet — ✅ 2026-07-31
- [ ] TASK-0126: Maintainer review — `VectorDBAdapter` gained `list_collections()` and `drop_collection()` under TASK-0040, with a vendor-neutral `CollectionInfo` result. `adapters/` is a folder-boundary-sensitive package; the addition is judged to strengthen the boundary rather than weaken it, because the documented `fasterrag index list` and `index delete` are otherwise unimplementable without the CLI reaching into a vendor client directly. Both are covered by the shared contract suite. Confirm or revert
- [x] TASK-0127: The semantic cache is unusable from the CLI: `cache.backend` accepts only `memory` and `redis`, and a memory cache dies with each short-lived CLI process, so no two `fasterrag query` invocations can ever share one. Verified in-process instead (16.7s cold vs 69ms on a hit against a real corpus). TASK-0124's redis backend resolves it; until then consider documenting in cookbook.md that the semantic cache benefits `fasterrag serve` and the library, not one-shot CLI calls — `cache.backend` now accepts `disk`, the only backend that survives between two invocations of a command-line tool. `memory` remains the default for the server, and `redis` still fails fast until TASK-0124 — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0216: The per-key rate limiter was an in-process fixed window, so N replicas granted N times the configured limit — ✅ 2026-11-02 (extracted behind a `RateLimiter` ABC with two implementations: `InProcessRateLimiter`, which is the existing dictionary and stays the **default** so nothing changes for a single-process user, and `RedisRateLimiter`, which counts one fixed window per key in Redis so every replica shares one ceiling. INCR and EXPIRE travel in one `transaction=True` pipeline, because a process dying between them leaves an immortal counter that throttles a key forever. The identity is hashed into the key rather than stored, so an API key never becomes a Redis key. **A Redis outage fails open** — the limiter is an abuse control, not an authorization control, and failing closed would turn a Redis outage into a total control-plane outage for every authenticated caller including the admin endpoints an operator needs during that incident; the same posture FMEA row 23 takes for this dependency. The failure is never swallowed: each occurrence logs a WARNING carrying the request's `trace_id`. The pool is released from the lifespan scope in a `finally`, the only place a middleware-owned resource can be closed. **Untested against a live server** — the tests drive an in-memory double, which can prove which commands are issued and that they share a transaction, and cannot prove redis-py and a real server agree with the double; security.md says so outright and TASK-0290 carries the gap)

- [ ] TASK-0079: Implement export/import portability archives + vector-copy and re-embed migration paths (D11) — **in progress**. The read side landed 2026-08-03: `VectorDBAdapter.iterate_points` walks a whole collection through Qdrant's scroll cursor, returning vendor-neutral `Point`s with the original fasterRag id restored from the payload and named dense vectors unwrapped. An async iterator rather than a list, because a collection is precisely the thing that does not fit in memory. 10 tests cover the cursor loop, multi-page streaming, exhaustion, both vector layouts, and error translation — but against a fake client only: **Docker was down, so nothing has iterated a live collection yet** (same blocker as TASK-0178). The **archive writer** landed next: `export_archive` streams rows through a staging directory rather than assembling them in memory — a collection is exactly the thing that does not fit — and writes the manifest last because it carries counts that only exist once the rows do. Members get a fixed mtime and owner so two exports of an unchanged collection are byte-identical, or every backup diff reports a timestamp and hides the real changes. Documents are reconstructed from chunk payloads rather than read from the journal, because the journal describes an ingestion *run* while the archive must describe the collection as it stands. An empty collection refuses to export: an empty archive imports cleanly and silently produces an empty collection. 15 tests. The **reader and both import paths** landed next: `open_archive` verifies format major, required members, checksums, manifest counts, and referential integrity *before* a caller can write anything — a half-imported collection looks like a corpus rather than an error, so the ordering is the safety property. It also refuses any tar member that is not a plain file at the archive root, rejecting the classic traversal at the boundary. A vector copy is permitted only when the archive carries vectors *and* model, model version, and dimensions all match, with every mismatch named rather than summarised. `--reembed` is always legal because it derives what it stores. `export` and `import` are now real CLI commands rather than `PENDING_COMMANDS` entries. 38 tests across writer and reader; `archive.py` at 100% coverage, `archive_import.py` at 91%. **Still unverified against a live backend** — no archive has been exported from or imported into a real Qdrant, and the D11 round-trip acceptance test cannot run until Docker is back (B7.2). `POST /v1/admin/export` and `/import` are now served, tenant-scoped, and covered by a round-trip test that exports and re-imports entirely over REST. **A real defect surfaced there**: a deployment with no lockfile and no configured `embeddings.dimensions` recorded `dimensions: 0` in the manifest, and an archive declaring zero dimensions cannot be imported at all — the export succeeded and produced something unusable. Both sides now fall back to the width actually observed in the data. The reverse-drift half of the OpenAPI gate earned its keep here too: it caught `api-reference.md` still marking both endpoints **Not yet implemented** after they shipped. **~~What remains is verification, not construction~~ **(that was wrong — see the 2026-11-03 entry below)****: no archive has round-tripped through a real Qdrant, and the D11 acceptance test (Qdrant → Qdrant vector copy, Qdrant → pgvector re-embed) needs both a running daemon (B7.2) and the pgvector adapter (TASK-0049) — **UPDATE 2026-11-03: the claim above was false, and TASK-0214's "the whole path works" with it. Three defects survived the live acceptance run.** The vector-copy guard took the model and model version *out of the manifest* and asked the reader whether they matched the manifest — it compared the archive to itself and could never fire, so an archive of model A imported into a deployment configured for model B copied the vectors in silently. The function was well unit-tested in isolation, which is exactly why the tautology at its call site survived. Import verified `documents.jsonl` and then discarded it, so `source_uri` and `content_hash` — which the exporter deliberately lifts off the chunk into the document row — were lost on every round trip, leaving restored chunks uncitable and undedupable; **the live test missed it because its fixture used the key `source`, which the indexer never writes**. A write failing partway left a populated incomplete collection with no typed error, and spec rule 5's `index.lock` was never written at all. All four are fixed, the manifest's declared vector width is now verified against the vectors it describes, and `tests/unit/services/test_archive_format_spec.py` pins the archive against the tables in `docs/archive-format.md` in both directions rather than only against itself — 18 mutations, all red. **Every one of these fixes is proven against a fake adapter only**: all eight integration tests, including the corrected provenance assertion, skip for want of a Docker daemon, so nothing has round-tripped through a real Qdrant since the change. **Stays open**: `golden-set.jsonl` reserved but never written, sparse vectors unexportable (TASK-0291), import not streamed (TASK-0293), and the Qdrant → pgvector `--reembed` acceptance leg still waiting on TASK-0049
- [x] TASK-0183: CI went red on the Linux leg with four `NotADirectoryError`s that pass on Windows. Not a test artifact: three degradation paths (`TraceStore.store`, `DiskStore.set`, `LockStore.write`) called `temporary.unlink(missing_ok=True)` from inside their own `except OSError` handler. When the parent is a file rather than a directory, Linux raises `NotADirectoryError` *from the cleanup*, replacing the error being handled with a second one that escapes — so a path designed to degrade crashed instead. Windows does not raise there, which is exactly why only the Linux leg caught it. Each cleanup is now wrapped in `suppress(OSError)`. Reproduced locally before fixing and confirmed the store degrades rather than raising — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [ ] TASK-0182: (maintainer review) `VectorDBAdapter` gained `iterate_points` under TASK-0079. It is the read side of portability and every future adapter must implement it — Qdrant has `scroll`, pgvector would be a cursor, but a backend with no scan primitive could not satisfy the contract at all. Same review as TASK-0126 and TASK-0133: confirm the addition before it hardens
- [x] TASK-0046: Implement security layer — **API-key auth with scopes and per-key rate limiting shipped**; `security.auth` has left the accepted-but-unenforced list. Enforced as ASGI middleware rather than per-route dependencies, so forgetting to protect a new router is impossible by construction and an unmapped path requires `admin` rather than defaulting to open. Auth runs before metrics so a rejected request cannot inflate counters. `AUTH_MISSING`/`AUTH_INVALID`/`AUTH_SCOPE`/`RATE_LIMITED` are RFC 9457 problems, an unknown key is indistinguishable from a revoked one, keys compare with `secrets.compare_digest` and no early exit, and enabling auth without a usable key refuses to start. 24 tests. **Multi-tenancy isolation is NOT included** and `security.multi_tenancy` still refuses to start — that half is TASK-0179 — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0179: Multi-tenancy isolation — the tenant is resolved once in `AuthMiddleware` from the key and the configured header, refusing a mismatch with `TENANT_FORBIDDEN` and refusing a missing header outright, since an unstated tenant would silently act on the untenanted namespace. A key with no tenant is an operator credential that may act as any tenant but must still name one, and a refusal never echoes the key's own tenant so tenants cannot be enumerated from the error. The **semantic cache is scoped on the entry, not the key**: lookup compares vectors across every stored entry, so a key prefix alone would not stop a similar question from one tenant returning another's answer — the owning tenant travels inside the entry and a mismatch is skipped. The tenant is in the key as well, or two tenants asking the same question collide and the second overwrites the first. `security.multi_tenancy` now starts. 11 tests. Collections, traces, and metric labels are not yet scoped — TASK-0180 — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0180: Finish tenant-tagging the remaining state — **collections, traces, and metric labels**. Collections are scoped by *name* rather than by a filter, because a vector database has no notion of our tenants: a `<tenant>__` prefix makes isolation a property of the identifier, so a missing check fails closed instead of open. The prefix never reaches a response, and a tenant id containing the separator is refused — `acme__x` could otherwise address `acme`'s collection `x`. Another tenant's trace or collection is reported **absent, not forbidden**, since a distinct error confirms the id is real and trace ids are guessable from a caller's own listing. Metric series take the tenant the middleware resolved rather than one re-read from the header, so an unauthenticated request cannot label its own series. 24 tests. Ingestion jobs and the journal are still unscoped — TASK-0181 — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0181: Scope ingestion jobs and the journal to their tenant — `load_job(job_id, tenant=...)` raises the same `NOT_FOUND` for another tenant's job as for an unknown id, with an identical message, because a job record carries the source paths of the corpus and job ids sort chronologically, so a caller holding one of their own can guess at neighbours. **Found while wiring it: `start_ingest` never recorded the tenant at all** — scoping the read path alone would have refused every tenant access to the job it had just started, presenting as a total outage rather than as isolation. Creation now records it. 6 tests, including one asserting the refusal is byte-identical to an unknown id — ✅ 2026-08-03

### S9 — Trace store + replay

- [x] TASK-0041: Implement OTel instrumentation (retrieval/reranker/context-assembly/generation trace types, trace-id correlation) — the four span types are recorded against one clock origin and correlated by trace id; OTLP export itself is TASK-0132 — ✅ 2026-08-01
- [x] TASK-0042: Implement metrics catalogue export (RED, per-stage latency, tokens, cost/query, cache ratio, queue/DLQ depth, breaker state) — every documented metric declared and served at `GET /metrics`; the gauges fed by the worker pools and circuit breaker are declared but only populated once those slices report into them — ✅ 2026-08-01
- [x] TASK-0080: Implement local trace store + fasterrag replay with side-by-side retrieval/answer diff (D8) — ✅ 2026-08-01
- [x] TASK-0131: `fasterrag traces list|show` was added beyond `docs/cli-reference.md`, because a 32-hex trace id is unreachable without a way to list recent ones — ✅ 2026-11-02 (confirmed against the source: the commands exist in `cli/commands/traces.py`, are registered in `cli/parser.py` and dispatched in `cli/main.py`, and `--help` matches. The doc was accurate but incomplete, so three things were added rather than corrected: `--limit`'s default of 20 — the store's own default is 50, but the CLI always passes the flag, so 20 is what an operator sees; the `--json` shapes; and the exit codes, which were missing entirely — 0 for a successful `list` *including an empty one*, 1 when `show` names a trace that is not stored, 2 when the config fails to load. That an empty `list` is a success, and prints `(traces.store is false)` when storage is off, is recorded too, so an empty result can never be read as a broken store)
- [x] TASK-0132: OTel export is not wired. The four RAG spans are recorded and persisted by the trace store, which is what replay and the dashboard read, but `observability.otel: true` does not yet emit them over OTLP to `observability.otel_endpoint`. Needs the opentelemetry SDK dependency and an exporter — ✅ 2026-08-05 (`observability/otel_export.py` maps a `Trace` onto a root span with a child per stage and ships it over OTLP/HTTP. **The fasterRag trace id is preserved** — it is already 32 hex characters, exactly the OpenTelemetry shape, so the id in a `problem+json` body, the logs, `GET /v1/traces/{id}`, and a trace viewer's search box are one id; minting a separate one would leave an operator holding a failing request's id with no way to find it. Span ids are derived from the trace id and stage name so a retried export is one trace rather than two overlapping copies. Verified beyond the mapping: the spans encode as real OTLP protobuf, and a local HTTP receiver confirmed the full wire path — a POST to `/v1/traces` with `application/x-protobuf`, trace id intact on the wire, stage durations exact. Ships as the optional `otel` extra; a missing SDK warns rather than refusing to serve queries, and an unreachable collector costs the record and nothing else. `TraceStore` now holds a list of exporters instead of one Langfuse slot, so Langfuse and OTLP can both be on. **Not verified against a real collector** — Jaeger or an OTel Collector needs Docker, which is blocker B7)
- [x] TASK-0189: Export metrics over OTLP as well as spans. `observability.otel: true` currently ships the four RAG spans only; the metric catalogue is still Prometheus-scrape-only via `/metrics`, so a deployment whose observability stack is OTLP-native sees fasterRag's traces but none of its counters. Needs the SDK's meter provider and a periodic reader wired to the same endpoint — ✅ 2026-08-05 (`observability/otel_metrics.py` pushes the catalogue every 60s, started and stopped in the API lifespan with a final snapshot on shutdown so the last window is not lost. **One source of truth**: `Registry.snapshot()` returns typed `Sample`s that both the OTLP payload and the Prometheus text are built from, rather than one parsing the other's output. **The mapping hazard is the histogram**: Prometheus buckets are cumulative and OTLP explicit-bucket histograms are per-bucket, so sending ours unchanged would have inflated the counts — measured 3.75× on a six-bucket example — and every percentile read off the OTLP copy would have disagreed with the same percentile read off the scrape, with the OTLP one wrong. Converted, and pinned by a test asserting per-bucket counts sum to the observation count. Verified end to end: a local receiver confirmed the wire path, and the real app with the toggle on pushed a snapshot whose `requests_total` matches the scrape exactly at the same instant. **Not verified against a real collector** — that needs Docker, blocker B7)

Same boundary question as TASK-0126; confirm or revert

### S11 — Chaos, load, soak; baselines

- [x] TASK-0083: Implement the scripted chaos suite (kill-worker, stop-Qdrant, corrupt-doc, slow-LLM, disk-full) and degradation ladder verification (D4/D12) — all five scenarios scripted and passing; observed behavior recorded in failure-modes.md — ✅ 2026-08-01
- [x] TASK-0135: The chaos suite injects the stop-Qdrant and disk-full faults at the adapter and filesystem seams rather than by stopping a real container or filling a real disk. That verifies fasterRag's response but not the operating system's reporting of those conditions; add container-stop and disk-quota variants under the `integration` marker — ✅ 2026-08-09 (`tests/chaos/test_real_faults.py`, 10 cases, `chaos` + `integration`. **Both variants are real; neither is simulated.** A throwaway Qdrant on its own name, ports, and volume is really stopped with `docker stop`, and the real journal write path runs inside a Linux container whose journal directory is a tmpfs with a hard 512k limit, filled to zero free bytes — a genuine filesystem returning genuine `ENOSPC`, not an unwritable directory. The OS-level disk variant could not be built on the Windows host itself: constraining an NTFS volume needs a mounted VHD or a disk quota and both require administrator rights a test must never demand, so the container is the honest substitute and the case runs unchanged on Linux or macOS. Delivered by salvaging an abandoned worktree — what shipped, what was disproven, and the four mutations are recorded under TASK-0233, and the one live defect it exposed under TASK-0234)
- [ ] TASK-0136: Recovery time per chaos scenario is not measured — D12's proof metric asks for it, and it needs the isolated hardware TASK-0084 is blocked on
- [x] TASK-0137: The error-code table has no timeout code for generation. Embeddings distinguish `EMBED_PROVIDER_TIMEOUT` from `EMBED_PROVIDER_ERROR`, but a generation timeout can only be reported as `GENERATION_FAILED`, so `reliability.timeouts.llm_ms` firing is indistinguishable from any other provider failure in logs and metrics. Consider `LLM_TIMEOUT`, alongside TASK-0125 — added `GENERATION_TIMEOUT` (504, retryable), mirroring the embedding split. A timeout says raise `reliability.timeouts.llm_ms` or shrink the context; a hard failure says look at the provider, and one code for both cannot say which — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0048: Implement benchmark suite per performance.md (p50/p95, throughput, cache hit rate, cost/query; --ledger output) — ingest and query suites; the eval suite needs TASK-0077's golden set — ✅ 2026-08-01

Run-to-run variance exceeded the effect being measured (p50 5.5 s vs 8.7 s across two invocations of the same commit), and cold start came out *faster* than the warmed median — both symptoms of the missing isolation. slo.md's TBDs stay TBD until superseding entries come from a quiet machine

Steps 1–3 (clean host, `.env`, doctor) and step 6 (`index lock verify`) did not run, because the restore was onto the same host — that makes it the §4 single-collection shortcut, not the §2 clean-host drill. RPO/RTO stay TBD: the measured 4,311 ms is a restore duration for one tiny collection, not `T1 − T0` from a clean host. Execution log in disaster-recovery.md
- [x] TASK-0138: Implement backup/restore for the remaining §4 shortcuts and a documented default schedule (daily snapshots retained 14 days); today `fasterrag backup` is a manual command with no cadence, so the documented default is a statement about tooling that does not exist yet — ✅ 2026-08-06 (**the defect was worse than the task described**. `back_up` wrote straight into the destination, so every run overwrote the previous manifest: a deployment running daily backups held exactly *one* recovery point, not fourteen, and would have discovered that only when the newest backup turned out to be the corrupt one. Worse, the overwritten manifests were the only record of their backend snapshots, which stayed inside the vector database orphaned and invisible, consuming disk forever. Reproduced before fixing: two runs, two snapshots taken, one referenced, one leaked. Each run now writes a timestamped set, `--retain N` (default 14) prunes older sets *and* deletes the snapshots they reference, and `restore` accepts either one set or the destination. Verified: 6 runs at retain=3 leave 3 sets, 3 snapshots deleted, and **0 orphans**. Retention counts sets rather than days, because fasterRag cannot know the cadence a scheduler was configured with. **Cadence was deliberately not built** — cron, systemd timers, and Task Scheduler already do it better and are what an operator monitors; a scheduler inside a RAG framework would be a worse one that fails silently. `docs/disaster-recovery.md` now carries a crontab line instead of claiming tooling that should not exist. Two further gaps found: the backup service **had no tests at all**, which is how the overwrite survived — 17 now cover sets, retention, orphans, corrupt manifests, and restore resolution; and `fasterrag backup`/`restore` were **entirely absent from cli-reference.md** despite the parser claiming the surface mirrors it exactly. `VectorDBAdapter` gained `delete_snapshot`, which retention cannot work without — same folder-boundary review as TASK-0126/0133/0182, filed as TASK-0191)
- [x] TASK-0192: Cover `IngestionService.run` at the unit level — ✅ 2026-08-06 (a coverage audit over the whole tree found the primary write path at **31%**: source resolution, both worker pools, journal checkpointing, dead-lettering, the lockfile, cache invalidation, and the ingestion metrics were exercised **only** by a Docker-gated integration test. With the daemon down all session that path had not executed once, and CI's unit leg never touches it — so every change routed through it this session, including the late-chunking document carry and the circuit breaker, landed without the orchestration being run. Now **92%**, with 15 tests over a faked backend: completion, dedup on replay, dead-lettering for both an unreadable source and a failed provider, checkpoint resume, cache invalidation *and* the case where nothing was indexed so the warm cache must be left alone, metrics, metadata, tenancy, and idempotency replay. The existing `RecordingAdapter` is reused rather than a second partial double written, so a change to the adapter contract breaks one place)
- [x] TASK-0193: Cover the LLM adapters' call paths — ✅ 2026-08-06 (`adapters/llm/openai.py` sat at 22%: the existing suite covered construction, the factory, and failure classification, but never called `complete` or `stream`, so request building and response parsing went unexercised — the two places a provider integration is most often wrong and the two that otherwise only fail against a live API. Now 83%, with 20 tests over a faked client: message ordering, sampling settings reaching the request, token usage carried through (the cost metric multiplies these, so a dropped count reads as a free query), a null `content` becoming an empty answer rather than crashing every consumer, truncation reported, streaming asking for usage, the usage-only final event skipped rather than yielded as a blank token, and vendor exceptions becoming typed errors on both call shapes. **Verified the tests have teeth by mutation** rather than trusting the coverage number: appending the system message *after* the user message — a real bug that providers accept silently — and yielding empty deltas each fail exactly one test, and only that test)
- [x] TASK-0194: (security) `POST /v1/replay` looked its trace up **unscoped**, so any authenticated tenant could replay another tenant's trace — ✅ 2026-08-06 (found by auditing `api/traces.py`, the one API module below 90% coverage at 44%. `GET /v1/traces/{id}` and `GET /v1/traces` both passed `tenant=` to `TraceStore.load`/`recent`; replay passed nothing, so the store applied no filter. Replay re-executes the stored question and returns a diff, so the disclosure is the other tenant's query text and retrieved chunks — the exact content the read endpoints refuse, reached through a write-shaped endpoint. **Reproduced against the running API before fixing**, with auth and multi-tenancy on and two tenant-bound keys: `evilcorp` got 404 on both GETs and reached the trace on replay. Fixed by scoping the lookup, and confirmed: `evilcorp` now gets 404 while the owner still reaches its own. 10 tests added, and the key one was verified to fail against the unfixed code rather than assumed to. **Severity depends on configuration** — it only bites a deployment running `security.auth: true` *and* `security.multi_tenancy: true`, which is not the default and is a slice that shipped recently, so exposure is likely small. No other unscoped lookup exists: the dashboard and both read endpoints were already correct)
- [x] TASK-0195: Pin every registered route's required scope as a reviewable table — ✅ 2026-08-06 (follow-on from TASK-0194. `required_scope` was tested in isolation, but nothing asserted the mapping it produces for the routes the application *actually registers*. An unmapped route inherits `admin` by omission, which is the safe direction; the unsafe ones are a route landing under an existing prefix and silently inheriting that prefix's weaker scope, or a path being added to `PUBLIC_PATHS`. Neither leaves any other signal in a diff. The full inventory was audited first and is correct — 21 routes, three public probes, everything else scoped as intended, `/v1/estimate` on `admin` because it parses server-side paths. **Both failure directions were verified to trip the tripwire**: a new `GET /v1/query/debug` (which would inherit `query`) fails the inventory test, and moving `/metrics` into `PUBLIC_PATHS` fails two. Walking nested routers matters here — `app.routes` holds only `/openapi.json` in this FastAPI version, so a naive implementation would pass while inspecting nothing)
- [x] TASK-0198: Audit every CLI flag for one nothing reads — ✅ 2026-08-06 (generalising TASK-0188, where `--recursive` was declared, documented, and read nowhere, so directory ingestion silently indexed nothing. Comparing all 63 declared argparse destinations against every consumer found **five more in the same state**: `--show-chunks`, `--no-stream`, `--priority-class`, `--watch`, and `doctor --fix` — each accepted by argparse and documented in cli-reference.md as a working feature. Three are now implemented: `--show-chunks` prints the candidate set via `answer_with_candidates`, `--priority-class` sets the `priority_class` metadata the tiering rules already match on, and `--no-stream` means something because the CLI now *streams by default* — `run_query` only ever called `answer()`, so its own docstring claiming it streamed 'unless --no-stream was given' was false. `--json` and `--show-chunks` imply `--no-stream`, since a partial document is not a document. The two that cannot be built yet say so in `--help` **and** print a notice at the point of use, because help text is read once while the notice reaches whoever actually passed the flag. Filed as TASK-0196 (`--watch`, needs the queue-backed job path) and TASK-0197 (`doctor --fix`, needs Docker). A guard test now fails on any newly declared flag nothing reads; verified by adding one)
- [x] TASK-0196: Implement `ingest --watch` and `index reembed --watch`. Both declare the flag and print a not-implemented notice today. The CLI runs its job inline to completion, so there is nothing to follow until ingestion moves behind the queue-backed job path (TASK-0130) — ✅ 2026-10-25 (**the premise in this ledger line was wrong, and finding that out was the task.** There IS followable state during an inline run: `CpuWorkerPool` appends a `DocumentRecord` to the journal as each document settles and `Journal.counts()` reads it, so `--watch` never depended on TASK-0130. Implemented as live per-document progress of the inline job: the job id is emitted up front via `service.accept`, a follower polls the journal and appends a `progress` line whenever the tally moves, and the flag refuses cleanly under `--json`, `--quiet` and `--dry-run`. The remote-polling reading was rejected because the CLI has no remote mode at all — grep for remote/api_url/base_url/httpx under `cli/` returns nothing — so it would have meant inventing a client first. **Three defects came back from adversarial review.** (1) A real bug: `_run_watched`'s `finally` cancelled the follower and awaited it, but `cancel()` is a no-op on an already-failed task, so the await re-raised and *discarded a successful ingest* — the operator saw a traceback for a job that had completed. A failure in the progress reporter can no longer destroy the result it is reporting on; it is logged against the job id and rendered as a typed problem. (2) A vacuity: `--watch` was the last entry in the pending-flag registry, so emptying it turned three tests into unconditional passes that were counted in the evidence; they now skip visibly with a reason naming the vacuity. (3) `_follow_job`'s docstring claimed the loop 'costs one journal read per interval however large the corpus is' — unmeasured, and wrong in direction, since `Journal.counts()` re-parses the whole growing document log on every poll. Rewritten as mechanism. Also: `progress indexed=N` counted documents while the summary's `indexed M chunks` counted chunks, so a watched run ended with two different 'indexed' numbers; one is renamed)
- [x] TASK-0197: Implement `doctor --fix` — ✅ 2026-08-11 (unblocked by a working Docker daemon, and every claim below was reproduced against one. **Two repairs qualify, and the bar is that a repair must be idempotent, non-destructive, and unsurprising**: `docker volume create` for the missing named storage volume (converges — an existing volume is returned untouched, so an index can never be reinitialised), and `docker start` for fasterRag's *own* stopped container. Everything else keeps its concrete fix-it line and is reported `needs human` with the reason: freeing a port means killing whatever holds it, freeing disk means deleting files fasterRag did not write, and `.env` and `config.yaml` are the operator's to author — a diagnostic that rewrote the config would destroy the intent it was run to explain. **Creating** a container is deliberately excluded even though it would make more reports go green: it pulls an image and publishes ports, which is `provision qdrant`'s job. **Every attempt is classified from a second full run of the report, never from the repair returning without an error** — trap T23, the T12 defect one layer up, since "created the volume" is not evidence the check now passes; the mutation that scores from the attempt instead turns `test_a_repair_that_did_not_take_is_reported_not_fixed` red. Starting a container needed a new failing check to hang off, so `vector_db_volume` was added: a missing volume is otherwise invisible until the container is replaced and the index is silently gone. It does **not** block provisioning (`provision qdrant` creates the volume, so gating on it would refuse the command that fixes it), and when the daemon is down it is **omitted rather than passed** — the docker check already owns that root cause, and a check reporting PASS on a probe that never ran is exactly T12. The safety guard is the `fasterrag.managed=true` label: a container holding the configured name without it belongs to somebody else and is left alone (trap T24), and because that guard is worthless if the string written at `docker run` ever drifts from the one read at `docker inspect`, an integration test pins the two together against a real daemon. Exit codes are unchanged — `0` when everything passes after the repairs, `4` when anything still fails — so `--fix` cannot launder a failed preflight into a green one. **Reproduced against real broken states, not stubs**: a real absent volume (doctor reported it, `--fix` created it, the re-check confirmed it, a second run reported `rechecked: false`), and the real managed container stopped with `docker stop` (`--fix` started it, waited for readiness, and the re-check read `vector_db: reachable in 59.556 ms`, exit 0); both were restored and every probe volume and container removed. **Eight mutations, each proving a named test**: dropping the management-label, absent-container, and already-running guards; scoring an attempt without the re-check; passing the volume check when Docker is down; taking the exit code from the pre-fix report; repairing without `--fix`; and drifting the label written from the label read. **Salvaged from the abandoned worktree `agent-af26b20ddf611b53b` semantically, never copied** — it was written against a much older `main`. Its three-status vocabulary, the `FixOutcome` before/after shape, and the `as_dict` superset rule survived review; **rejected** were its `_check_vector_db_volume` returning a *passing* "not checked" check when Docker is down (T12), its per-attempt status taken from whether the fixer raised rather than from the re-check (the defect this task exists to avoid), its `volume_exists` docstring claiming a down daemon raises when `docker volume inspect` merely exits non-zero, and its unit fixture, which never stubbed `volume_exists` and so shelled out to a real daemon — passing on a developer machine that happened to have the volume and failing outright in CI, which has none)
- [x] TASK-0258: Give `doctor --fix` a REST counterpart, or record the asymmetry as deliberate — ✅ 2026-10-28 (built, after the decision was recorded on 2026-10-25. `POST /v1/admin/doctor/fix` calls `services.doctor.apply_fixes` — the same function the CLI reaches — so the two repairs, the `fasterrag.managed=true` container guard, and the rule that every attempt is classified from a **second** doctor report rather than from a repair returning without raising are shared by construction instead of by parallel implementation. No request body: the API serves one validated `Settings`, and letting a caller point repairs at an arbitrary config file on the server's disk would be more authority than the parity argument asks for. Always `200`, since a repair that did not hold is the answer and `passed: false` is the twin of the CLI's exit code 4. `GET /v1/admin/doctor` stays read-only and its pinning test still passes. Six endpoint tests over a faked Docker boundary cover the label guard, the needs-a-human classification, and a repair that returns cleanly while leaving its check failing (T12/T23). Mutation-verified: deleting the route fails five endpoint tests plus the frozen route map; classifying from the repair instead of the re-check fails two; skipping the label check fails one)
- [x] TASK-0199: (security) `security.max_request_mb` was declared, documented, and enforced by nothing — ✅ 2026-08-06 (found by auditing all 108 schema settings against every consumer, the same declaration-versus-consumer check that found the dead CLI flags. `docs/security.md` §5 states 'Input size limits on all endpoints: `security.max_request_mb` (bodies)... oversize → 413 PAYLOAD_TOO_LARGE'. **Reproduced: with the limit set to 1 MB, an 8 MB body answered 200** — request bodies were entirely unbounded, which is a memory-exhaustion vector and a security control that read as configured while doing nothing. Implemented as ASGI middleware added last so Starlette wraps it outermost and it runs *before* authentication — checking after auth would still let an unauthenticated caller make the server read the whole body. Two checks, because either alone is bypassable: `Content-Length` refused before the body is read at all, and a running byte count for chunked uploads, which omit the header entirely. The count raises rather than truncating, since a handler handed a short read would answer as though the request were complete. 10 tests, including the chunked path a header-only check would miss. Three further settings are read by nothing and are now listed as deliberate, each against a task: `reliability.degradation_ladder` (TASK-0165), `cost.estimator` (TASK-0200), `autopilot.golden_set_size` (TASK-0201). A guard test fails on any new setting nothing reads, and on a pending entry whose task todo.md does not list)
- [x] TASK-0200: `cost.estimator` is declared and read by nothing — ✅ 2026-08-09 (gated rather than dropped, because config-reference.md sells the setting as the switch for D9. **Reproduced on `b76f811` before any fix**: with `cost.estimator: false`, `fasterrag estimate` exited `0` and priced the corpus, `POST /v1/estimate` answered `200` with a full estimate, `ingest --dry-run` printed 'would index 1 chunks from 1 documents', and `FasterRag.estimate` returned 9 tokens — four surfaces, none of them reading the switch. `require_estimator(settings)` lives in `services/estimation.py` and is called by all four. It refuses with `VALIDATION_FAILED` naming `cost.estimator`, never an estimate of zero: a report of no tokens and no cost is indistinguishable from a corpus that is free to ingest. CLI surfaces exit `2`, the endpoint answers `422` problem+json. **One deliberate exemption, pinned by its own test**: `benchmark --suite ingest` calls `estimate_sources` to *time* parse-and-chunk and reports no cost — verified by reading `_ingest_suite`, which uses the result only for durations and a `documents/tokens` dataset label — so the gate sits at the entry points rather than inside the function. 10 tests across the service, both CLI commands, the endpoint, and the facade; each was mutation-tested by reverting its gate and watching it fail. Removed from the `PENDING` dict in `test_settings_are_wired.py`; it was never in `loader.py::_reject_unenforced_settings` and must not be, since it defaults to `true` and that list refuses startup on a non-default value)

Two tests now assert the parsers found something at all, since a pattern matching nothing reports perfect parity against an empty set)

What is verified is the gate. `tests/eval/test_live_provider_gating.py` deliberately carries **no** `eval` marker, so it runs in the `not integration and not eval` job — the job that cannot skip — with 22 tests over absent/blank/whitespace/present credentials, endpoint scheme accept-and-reject, model default vs override vs no-model, and the rule that a credential value never reaches a skip message or the ledger. - [x] TASK-0206: Report a truncated answer and dropped evidence — ✅ 2026-08-06 (found while validating `openai_compatible` against a real third-party endpoint. Both are now surfaced: the response carries `truncated` (the model stopped at `llm.max_tokens`) and `evidence_dropped` (chunks retrieved and reranked that did not fit the context budget — nothing cut off, but the answer is grounded in a subset of what retrieval chose). A truncated completion logs a warning naming whether the answer came back empty; dropped evidence logs the counts, which is what tells an operator to raise `chunking.chunk_size` or lower `retrieval.top_k`. The same probe confirmed the wire contract holds for a *second* vendor: base_url honoured, usage reported, streaming functional — though Gemini returns the whole answer as one event, so streaming buys no time-to-first-token there, unlike OpenAI's 1.2s against 4.2s. A guard now catches a computed property nothing reads, the variant the flag, setting, and schema-field guards all miss. **It matches by attribute name, not by type**, so two classes sharing a name mask each other — stated in the test rather than left as a silent weakness)

Second, the embedding cache is a *cost* control, and unit tests can only prove it returns the stored vector — not that the provider was never reached, which is the thing being paid for. **Confirmed**: a repeat of two passages sent zero further texts and took 0 ms against 5.7 s cold, and a partial hit sent only the one new passage while placing the cached vector back in its original position — a reassembly bug there would silently pair texts with another text's vector, which no unit test using a fake provider would distinguish from correct behaviour. - [x] TASK-0208: Apply the token-budget pass to every chunking strategy, not just `recursive` — ✅ 2026-08-06 (**TASK-0114 shipped incomplete and the tests did not notice**. The budget pass lived on `RecursiveChunker` alone, so `chunking.strategy: fixed` and `chunking.strategy: layout` still emitted chunks **3.6x their configured budget** on dense text — 166 tokens against a 46 limit — which is the exact defect TASK-0114 was written to fix. `late` was fine only because it wraps `recursive`. The pass now lives in `models.within_budget`, shared by all four, and the property tests fail for all four when it is disabled. Found by extending the chunking invariants to dense text: the existing properties generate ASCII only (codepoints 32–126), so the one input class the budget pass exists for was never generated. **The first version of that extension was vacuous and I nearly shipped it** — its alphabet included whitespace, so separator splitting broke every run into small pieces and the budget pass never triggered; disabling the pass failed none of the new tests. The strategy now excludes whitespace and sets a minimum length above the character limit, which is stated in a `# CRITICAL:` on the strategy itself. The lesson is the general one: a property test is worth exactly the inputs it generates, and the only way to know is to break the code and watch it fail)
- [x] TASK-0209: Property-test the identity invariants and close the digest's field-boundary collision — ✅ 2026-08-06 (document and chunk ids drive deduplication (D3) and lockfile drift (D1), where a collision is silent data loss — two distinct documents become one and the second is dropped as a duplicate. The 14 example tests covered the properties with one hand-picked collision pair; generated inputs found a **real one**: `_digest` joins its parts with a NUL, which separates only while no part contains a NUL, so `document_id('a' + NUL + 'b', 'c')` and `document_id('a', 'b' + NUL + 'c')` reached the same id. **Not exploitable today** — the tenant pattern rejects NUL and the other three `_digest` call sites pass hex digests, digits, or serialized config — but an invariant enforced only by a validator two modules away is one refactor from being false. `normalise_source` now refuses a NUL outright, which no filesystem or URL scheme permits anyway. Chosen over a length-prefixed encoding because that changes **every id ever minted** and so belongs at a release boundary with `IDENTITY_VERSION` bumped; the reasoning is recorded on `_digest` (TASK-0210). 12 property tests over unrestricted text, and the separator is written `chr(0)` rather than as an escape so the byte cannot land in the source file literally — which happened twice while writing this, once in the module and once in the test)
- [x] TASK-0210: (maintainer decision, release boundary) Replace the NUL-joined digest in `core/identity.py` with a length-prefixed encoding, removing the field-boundary constraint rather than policing it at the one call site that takes user input. It changes every document and chunk id, so it needs `IDENTITY_VERSION` bumped to 3 and a reindex; harmless now, impossible after the first tagged release — ✅ 2026-09-13 (maintainer authorized both this and TASK-0020 in one instruction. `_digest` now writes each part as `len:bytes` — the netstring form — and concatenates before hashing, so two distinct tuples cannot produce one byte string and a field may carry any byte at all. `IDENTITY_VERSION` is 3 with the history recorded on the constant; the lockfile drift check already consumed it dynamically, so no other code changed. `normalise_source` still refuses a NUL, now as plain input validation with the forgery reasoning removed from its message. **The first property written did not test what it claimed**: it split the joined string and re-hashed, which inserts an extra NUL and so never reproduces the version-2 collision — it passed against the old digest when mutation-checked. Rewritten to build the actual forgery, folding a NUL into a field to swallow its neighbour, and re-checked: fails against the old digest, passes against the new. Docs: release.md checklist ticked, release-readiness.md row closed, data-model.md scheme history, internals.md module map, CHANGELOG Changed entry. Full gate green: 2,360 unit tests, strict mypy on both platforms)
- [x] TASK-0211: (security) Two tenants could address one backend collection — ✅ 2026-08-06 (collection isolation is carried by the *name*, `<tenant>__<name>`, because a vector database has no notion of our tenants. Read *and* write, since the name is what the adapter upserts into — strictly worse than the replay disclosure (TASK-0194), which was read-only. A tenant id may now not end with `_`. With both rules the encoding is injective: a longer tenant sharing a prefix with a shorter one would need either the separator inside it or its first character at the end. **Two of my own test-design errors were caught while proving it.** The first strategy filtered tenants by restating the new rule, so reverting the fix left the generator unchanged and the injectivity property passed over inputs that could no longer collide; it now filters by *calling the validator*, so weakening the rule widens what is generated. The second: even widened, uniform search never finds a needle needing four correlated values, so the adversarial pair is constructed deliberately. Both failure modes are stated in the file, and reverting the fix now fails two tests rather than one)
- [x] TASK-0212: Property-test semantic cache isolation — ✅ 2026-08-06 (the third tenant-scoped surface, after the replay disclosure (TASK-0194) and the collection-name collision (TASK-0211). **No bug found** — the cache is correctly isolated, and is now proven so over generated vectors rather than asserted by a comment. The property that matters is the strongest form: an *identical* vector, similarity 1.0, must still not cross the boundary. It holds, including in a crowded cache where the scan walks other tenants' entries first, and `None` behaves as an owner rather than a wildcard. Removing the ownership check fails four of the eight tests. The design this confirms is worth recording: the check sits on the decoded *entry*, not the key, because lookup compares vectors across every stored entry and never reads keys — a key prefix alone would not isolate anything)
- [x] TASK-0213: Property-test context assembly — ✅ 2026-08-06 (assembly decides what evidence reaches the model, so a failure grounds an answer in something other than what the retriever chose. Eight of nine properties held: the budget binds, one citation per packed chunk, relevance order survives, duplicates are packed once, `empty` and `truncated` mean what they say. **The accounting did not reconcile.** A chunk carrying no text was skipped without being counted, so `used + dropped_duplicate + dropped_budget` came to less than the input — three chunks in, one accounted for — and a retrieved chunk could vanish with nothing reporting it. Now counted as `dropped_empty`. Our own chunkers guarantee non-empty chunks, so a non-zero value means the collection holds something they did not produce: an import, or a hand-written upsert. Low severity, but it is the kind of gap that makes a retrieval investigation start by doubting the numbers. The generator includes blank and whitespace-only text deliberately — a strategy that produced only real text could not have found it, which is the same mistake that made the first dense-chunking strategy vacuous (TASK-0208). The `

` separator in the assembled text is *not* re-split anywhere — `context.text` is only ever returned as the extractive answer — so the ambiguity a chunk containing the separator would create is not reachable, and was left alone rather than 'fixed')

Established precisely: every data member — `chunks.jsonl`, `documents.jsonl`, `vectors.jsonl`, `index.lock`, `checksums.sha256` — is byte-identical, and the manifest differs in that one field. - [x] TASK-0227: Stop Hypothesis's deadline making the property suite timing-dependent — ✅ 2026-08-09 (with the Docker fleet running, the full unit suite failed a *different* property test on each run — semantic-cache tenant isolation once, tenancy name scoping the next — each passing in isolation moments later. Cause: Hypothesis fails any example exceeding 200 ms by default, and every property in this repo asserts behaviour rather than latency. A correctness suite that goes red for reasons unrelated to correctness is worse than a slow one: it teaches whoever sees the failure to re-run rather than investigate, which is precisely the habit that would let a real isolation failure through — and both flaky tests here happen to be tenant-isolation properties. A `tests/conftest.py` profile disables the deadline; performance belongs in the benchmark ledger on isolated hardware, not as a side effect of a property test. A repeated id inside one leg was scored once per occurrence, so `rrf_fuse(["a", "b", "b"])` returned `["b", "a"]` — a single leg reordering its own input — and `rank_in` reported the document's *worst* position because the later write won. Separately, a leg weighted `0.0` still contributed every document it ranked at score `0.0`; `QueryService` truncates the fused list to `candidates` and hands the whole shortlist to the reranker, which can promote any of them, so a leg an operator had weighted out of the fusion could still put chunks in front of the model. Both verified on `main` before the fix and re-verified by mutation afterwards. Two claims made alongside them did **not** survive measurement and were rewritten rather than shipped: `math.fsum` was documented as repairing a leg-order bug, but the pre-fix code's running total cannot reorder anything `rrf_fuse` accepts — a single fusion's contributions span at most ~5e4 while reassociation needs ~4.5e15, confirmed by an exhaustive sweep of the permitted k/weight/position values (1.2M combinations) and a 2M-trial random search over a wider space. `fsum` is kept, because the margin belongs to the current config ranges rather than to the algorithm, but it is now documented as a guarantee held rather than a bug found. The zero-weight properties also reached their case by filtering with `assume`, which discarded 59% of examples (measured) and left them vacuous on the rest; they now construct a silenced leg instead.
