- [x] TASK-0229: Property-test the parsing layer and fix the eight defects the general forms exposed — ✅ 2026-08-09 (the example suite checked offsets, pages and sections on documents we wrote; this checks them on documents we did not, and every parser broke. **Text reaching the index:** a UTF-8 BOM survived `decode` because U+FEFF is not whitespace and `str.strip` keeps it — a BOM'd Markdown file lost every heading, a BOM'd CSV named its first column `﻿name`, and BOM'd JSON was rejected at line 1 column 1; the lenient fallback is a second `decode` call and needed the same fix. A whitespace-only CSV cell rendered as `column:   `, so a file of spaces and commas reached the index as a block of punctuation. A JSON `null` rendered as `": None"` and `""` as a bare `":"`. A `Heading 0` style gave a docx block outline depth 0, and since the builder keeps only entries strictly shallower than the incoming level, nothing is shallower than 0 — the entry never left the stack and every real h1 nested under it. **Errors escaping the taxonomy** (each a generic 500 with no problem+json body): `csv.Sniffer` can return a dialect whose delimiter equals its quote character, which `csv.reader` rejects with a plain `ValueError`, not even a `csv.Error`; `io.StringIO` translates newlines by default, so a lone CR raised `new-line character seen in unquoted field` on old-Mac files every spreadsheet opens; and any remaining `csv.Error` from the row loop is now a `ParseError`. **Resource:** `parse_document` read the whole file and then compared its length to `max_bytes`, admitting to memory the document the limit exists to keep out — the size now comes from `stat` and the file is never opened, verified by asserting `read_bytes` is not called. All eight reproduced on `main` first and all eight are mutation-verified. Two are unreachable by generation and are pinned as constructed cases: the `Heading 0` style, and a BOM in front of undecodable bytes. Both survived a mutation run before those tests were written, which is how they were found)

It reads as a hang, it lands on whichever document happens to arrive first, and because the cache is process-wide it made the queue-backpressure test pass or fail purely on whether an earlier test had already warmed it — the same order-dependent redness TASK-0227 dealt with on the Hypothesis side. `CpuWorkerPool.__aenter__` now warms the counter and logs the load when it exceeds 0.5 s, so the cost sits at startup where a slow start is expected. `test_the_pool_loads_the_tokenizer_before_it_accepts_work` guards it by name rather than leaving the backpressure test to fail confusingly on its behalf; both were mutation-verified against removing the warm-up)
- [x] TASK-0231: Derive the OTLP signal path from `otel_endpoint`, and verify both signals against a running collector — ✅ 2026-08-09 (closes the "not verified against a real collector" note left open by TASK-0132 and TASK-0189, and fixes what that verification was always going to find. **The defect: one setting fed two exporters and no value worked for both.** OTLP/HTTP puts each signal on its own path, and the SDK only appends that path for the `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` environment form — an endpoint passed explicitly is used verbatim, confirmed by reading `_endpoint` off both exporters. Each is a logged warning and nothing else, so the toggle reads as on while nothing arrives. `signal_endpoint()` now treats the setting as the collector's base URL, exactly as the OTel specification treats that environment variable, and replaces rather than appends a path that already names a signal so existing configurations keep working. Spans also gained the `fasterrag` instrumentation scope the metrics payload already carried; spans arriving with an empty scope are attributed to nothing. **Verified against `otel/opentelemetry-collector-contrib` 0.140.1**: both exporters returned success, the collector logged no warning, refusal, or rejection, and its debug output showed five spans on trace id `169a6d2c…` with `query` as root and the four stages parented to it, `InstrumentationScope fasterrag` on both signals, `fasterrag_requests_total` as a `Sum` with `IsMonotonic: true`, `fasterrag_queue_depth` as a `Gauge`, and `fasterrag_request_duration_seconds` bucket counts summing to its observation count of 2. Merged as semantic additions rather than copied, because copying would have reverted TASK-0226 and TASK-0190. **Three of its claims were stale or unmeasurable and did not ship.** (1) It asserted a stopped container reports `EMBED_PROVIDER_ERROR` and filed that as a defect; run against current `main` the assertion went **red** — the code is `RETRIEVAL_FAILED`, because TASK-0226 ✅ had already fixed it. The claim was dropped and the assertion corrected. (2) It filed `docker build` failing on the `.env.example` force-include; the `Dockerfile` already copies it, fixed under TASK-0190 ✅. Dropped. (3) It recorded the stopped-container failure as **78 ms**, which does not reproduce: four consecutive runs measured **44.2 ms, 5040.9 ms, 36.1 ms, 5043.9 ms** — bimodal, because Docker Desktop's port proxy sometimes accepts and hangs instead of the kernel refusing, in which case the bound is our own `reliability.timeouts.vector_db_ms` (5 s), not the OS's. The pinned figure was replaced with the assertion that actually holds, `elapsed < budget`, and the doc now records the range and the reason. It had also invented task ids — its `TASK-0216`/`TASK-0217`/`TASK-0218` are all taken on `main` by unrelated work. **Four mutations run, every one confirmed red before the suite was believed**: the stale error-code assertion failed on real code; disabling the probe's fill made the two ENOSPC cases fail; translating the journal's `OSError` made the defect-pinning case fail while leaving the loud-failure case green, which is the split those two are designed to have; and deleting the `.previous` rename from `_write_atomically` made the checkpoint case fail. That last one also **answered a question the salvaged probe never asked**: it defined `LATER_INDEX` and never used it, so nothing exercised a rewrite of an existing record. Measured, a *new* job cannot be written at `free_bytes: 0` while a *running* one can still checkpoint — `_write_atomically` renames the live record over the stale `.previous` copy first, freeing a file the size of the one it is about to write, and the mutation proves that is the mechanism. "Halt cleanly at a checkpoint" is therefore reachable on the very disk that caused the halt, which row 33 asserted and nothing had shown. Ten cases, `chaos` + `integration`, skipping cleanly without a daemon; the throwaway Qdrant uses its own name, ports 6733/6734, and volume so it can never touch the provisioner's)
- [x] TASK-0234: Translate a full filesystem into the typed error FMEA row 33 promises — ✅ 2026-08-10 (`Journal._write_atomically` let a raw `OSError` escape, so the row's "OS write error → typed `IngestionError`" was false and CLAUDE.md's mandatory taxonomy had a hole on the one path crash-resume depends on. Reproduced on `main` before touching it. The whole write — `mkdir`, the `.prev` rename, the write, and the publishing rename — is now wrapped, and a failure raises `IngestionError` chaining the original so **errno 28 survives translation**: a typed error that had forgotten it was `ENOSPC` would satisfy the taxonomy while telling an operator less than the raw error did, which is why the probe now records the chained cause and the test asserts both halves. Reported rather than swallowed-and-warned like a lost trace or lockfile, because losing a journal write quietly loses the ability to resume with nothing saying so. **The `.prev`-rename-before-write ordering was left exactly as it was** — TASK-0233 measured that it is what lets a running job checkpoint on the very disk that halted it, by freeing a file the size of the one about to be written. Verified against a real `ENOSPC` on a tmpfs, not only in simulation: the paired chaos assertion that TASK-0233 deliberately wrote to *expect the broken behaviour* was inverted in this same commit, per the T13 rule in rebuild-playbook.md, and reverting the translation turns it red again (9 passed, 1 failed). Two unit tests added; the first version of the temp-file-cleanup test was vacuous — patching `write_text` means no `.tmp` is ever created, so it passed with the cleanup deleted — and now injects the failure into the publishing rename instead. failure-modes.md row 33, rebuild-playbook.md T13, and release-readiness.md's gating row all moved with it)

Every agent worktree in this session therefore ran its own `tests/` against main's `src/` — and `pytest (unit)` is a pre-commit hook, so the gate agreed. The confusing direction is a new test for an unlanded fix failing; **the dangerous direction is a change whose behaviour main already had being committed green without its own code ever executing**, which is indistinguishable from a passing suite. Fixed by putting `src` first in `[tool.pytest.ini_options] pythonpath`, which pytest inserts at the front of `sys.path` ahead of the `.pth` entry. `tests/test_suite_targets_this_checkout.py` asserts the property directly rather than trusting it — it sits at the root of `tests/` because it is a property of the test *run*, not of any module. - [x] TASK-0249: Cover the untested CLI wrappers, and fix the seven defects that coverage exposed — ✅ 2026-08-09 (salvaged from the abandoned worktree `agent-a50d98db58d084a36`, based on `a0c5e16` while `main` had reached `8dff033`. Merged semantically per file rather than copied: a wholesale copy would have reverted TASK-0200's `require_estimator` guard, TASK-0201's `golden_set_size` fix and its test file, and TASK-0248's `pythonpath` change. `tests/unit/cli/` had no suite for `dispatch`, `serve`/`worker`, `backup`/`restore`, `export`/`import`, `benchmark`, `replay`, `traces`, or the golden-set generator; **+145 tests** now cover them, and the package gained the `__init__.py` every other test package already had. **Seven defects, each reproduced against `8dff033` before its fix was accepted, and every fix mutation-verified by reverting it and watching a named test go red.** (1) `benchmark --suite ingest --sources ./corpus --ledger` took the directory verbatim, so it timed one failed read and printed a pasteable `BENCH-0001` entry reading "Dataset: 0 documents, 0 tokens" beside a throughput figure — a published performance number backed by nothing, which is the exact thing the provable-claims policy exists to stop. Sources are expanded, and an expansion that finds nothing is refused with exit 2 rather than measured. (2) `estimate ./corpus` priced the whole corpus as one unreadable document and quoted every provider at $0.0000 while exiting 0; a preflight that does not price what the ingestion after it would read is not a preflight. Expansion runs after the `cost.estimator` refusal, because a disabled command has no business walking the filesystem. (3) `autopilot generate-golden-set ./corpus` handed the raw directory to the generator, the same TASK-0188 shape a third time. (4) `index reembed` already read `args.recursive`, which the parser declared nowhere — the handler was written for a flag argparse rejected with exit 2; `--recursive` is now declared on `reembed` and on `estimate`, matching `ingest`. (5) `index reembed --watch` was accepted and silently ignored, so TASK-0196's claim that "both declare the flag and print a not-implemented notice today" was true of `ingest` only; `test_flags_are_wired.py` could not catch it because it searches every CLI module at once, so `ingest`'s notice satisfied the assertion on `reembed`'s behalf. (6) `export` and `import` returned exit 1 for an unreachable backend, where every other command returns 3 and line 16 of `cli-reference.md` documents 3 as universal — the two commands a retry wrapper could not branch on. (7) `worker` reported `cpu=0` straight from `workers.cpu_pool_size`, documented as "0 = auto", while the pool it was about to start resolves 0 to the CPU count: measured, an operator was told 0 parse workers where 16 would run. **Three things were rejected or rewritten rather than salvaged.** The branch's `test_dispatch.py` was broken: `from fasterrag.cli import main as cli_main` binds the re-exported *function*, not the module, so all seven monkeypatches of `_HANDLERS`, `PENDING_COMMANDS`, and `_dispatch` raised `AttributeError` and asserted nothing. Its `--recursive` assertions built an `argparse.Namespace` by hand, which passes whether or not the parser declares the flag — exactly the defect under test — and now go through `build_parser`. And its benchmark test asserted only the reported dataset label, which the mutation run caught: reverting the expansion inside the *timed* closure left it green, because the label comes from a second call. `test_the_timed_work_is_the_expanded_corpus_and_not_the_directory` spies on every `estimate_sources` call, and fails under that mutation)
- [x] TASK-0257: Build the vector-DB circuit breaker — ✅ 2026-08-10 (the last outbound call on the query hot path with no shedding, and the one with nowhere to degrade to: a reranker failure drops to `hybrid_only` and a generation failure drops to `extractive`, but a retrieval failure means there is nothing to answer *from*, so it surfaces. That is exactly why the breaker belongs there — without one a backend that is down is searched again on every request, and those retries are the load keeping it down. `RetrievalService` now owns a `vector_db` breaker for its lifetime and guards the legs with it; `CircuitOpenError` already maps to a retryable 503 through the problem table, so shedding reports itself as the temporary condition it is. **A misattribution was caught while writing the test and fixed before it shipped**: the query embedding is an *embedding provider* call that happens to sit on the retrieval path, and it was inside the guarded region — an embedding outage would have opened the vector-database breaker, shedding reads from a backend that was perfectly healthy and hiding the real provider behind the wrong gauge. The embed now happens outside the guard, pinned by a test that fails when it is moved back. Four properties mutation-verified: the check, the failure record, the success record, and the misattribution. With this, reliability.md's "one breaker per provider (LLM, embeddings, vector DB)" is true for the first time — it was true for one of three when TASK-0245 was opened)

- [x] TASK-0259: Finish the abandoned pgvector pooling worktree — ✅ 2026-08-11 (salvaged from a worker stopped mid-verification, which is where the interesting part was. Its rendezvous helper `wait_until_blocked` was being passed the `blocker` connection — the one *holding* the lock — and the fixture's own CRITICAL comment explains why that cannot work: PostgreSQL runs `stats_fetch_consistency = cache`, so the statistics a transaction reads are frozen for its whole life, and polling from inside the transaction that holds the lock returns the pre-block snapshot forever. **The test did not pass vacuously — it failed honestly**, raising "no backend ever waited on the lock, so nothing in this case actually overlapped" rather than proving nothing quietly, which is the behaviour every concurrency test in this repo should have. It had built the separate autocommit `observer` fixture to fix it and had wired two of three call sites. The third is now wired. Also completed what it never reached: both settings documented in config-reference.md (the reference-parity gate was red on exactly those two keys), the example block updated since it claims to show all defaults, `ClassVar` annotations, and the two mutation runs. 50 integration cases green against a live PostgreSQL, including the shared adapter contract suite)
- [x] TASK-0260: Emit `estimated_cost_usd` in the query response body — ✅ 2026-08-28 (the costing side already existed — dated list-price tables with per-entry provenance, and `price_generation` already called on the generation path for the metric — so this was a response-shape change rather than a costing one. **The field is nullable and that is the substance of it**: a model with no recorded rate reports `null`, never `0.0`, because a zero reads as "this query was free" and that is a fabricated bill of exactly the kind the provable-claims policy exists to prevent. A local provider reports a genuine `0.0`, and the two cases are asserted separately so they cannot be conflated. Both surfaces carry it under the same name, so a streaming client needs no second code path — with a note at the streaming site that its counts are the local counter's rather than the provider's, since a stream never reports usage, making that figure an estimate of an estimate. Mutation-verified in both directions: never populating the field fails the local-provider case, and returning `0.0` for an unpriced model fails the null case. One test premise of mine was wrong and got fixed rather than worked around — the provider is the adapter's own attribute, not a settings value, so overriding `llm.provider` in settings changed nothing)

The vacuity this investigation turned up is TASK-0314, TASK-0315 and TASK-0316 — filed separately rather than buried in this note, because they were found by a different method and burying them is how findings get lost)
- [x] TASK-0262: Score Redis cache recency with a server-side sequence, not the client clock — ✅ 2026-08-12 (found by reproducing the `windows` CI job that TASK-0261 records as failing: `test_eviction_drops_the_least_recently_used` passes on this machine's Python 3.14 and fails on the 3.12 CI pins. **Measured rather than guessed**: `time.time()` resolves to 15.625 ms on Windows before CPython 3.13 — 2000 rapid calls returned **two** distinct values, against 1173 on 3.14 — so every entry touched inside one tick scored identically and Redis broke the sorted-set tie by comparing members lexicographically. **Eviction was alphabetical rather than least-recently-used**, and systematically: a hot key early in the alphabet was evicted again and again while a cold one late in it survived. That is a real defect on a supported platform, not a test artifact — `requires-python` is `>=3.12`. No epoch-based high-resolution clock exists there to switch to: `time`, `time_ns` and `monotonic` all returned a single distinct value in 2000 calls, and `perf_counter`, which is precise, is process-relative and so useless for a cache several replicas share. Recency is now an `INCR` on a per-namespace sequence key: strictly increasing, atomic, identical for every replica, and independent of any clock — which also removes a hazard the wall clock always had here and nothing had recorded, that Redis is the backend chosen *because* replicas share it and their clocks do not agree. Costs one round trip per touch. **The regression test was rewritten rather than reused**: the existing one cannot catch this on a modern interpreter, because a 100 ns clock makes the ties vanish, so a new case freezes `time.time()` outright and fails on every platform if recency ever returns to a wall clock — mutation-verified red on 3.12 *and* 3.14. Full suite green on both interpreters)
- [x] TASK-0263: Typecheck the Windows-only psycopg guard as both platforms — ✅ 2026-08-12 (the CI quality job's actual failure, finally reproduced: mypy narrows `sys.platform` to whichever platform it checks for, so `_require_compatible_event_loop`'s early `if sys.platform != "win32": return` made every line after it unreachable on Linux, and `warn_unreachable = true` turns that into an error. On Windows the same narrowing runs the other way and the code checks clean — which is why `mypy` passed for every person who ran it and failed in CI for eight days. Reproduced with `mypy --platform linux` once the possibilities had been eliminated one at a time: 3.12 versus 3.14 (both pass), a rich venv versus `.[dev]` (fixed separately, torch and transformers), and mypy 2.3.0 versus 2.3.1 (both pass). The check now sits inside `if sys.platform == "win32"`, a body mypy deliberately does not report as unreachable, and behaviour is unchanged — verified by running the guard under a real ProactorEventLoop, where it still raises, and a SelectorEventLoop, where it still does not)
- [x] TASK-0264: Sniff the CSV dialect independently of line endings — ✅ 2026-08-12 (found by a Hypothesis property that had never fired here, once the suite was finally run on the interpreter CI pins: `csv.Sniffer` detects a space delimiter for `'# LFLF# '` and then **fails outright on the identical file with CRLF**, falling back to a comma and splitting the rows differently. Windows line endings therefore changed what a block *was* rather than only what was inside it, which `test_crlf_and_lf_sources_differ_only_by_the_carriage_returns` asserts is impossible. Pre-existing rather than introduced — the sniff always saw raw text — and invisible on 3.13+, where the sniffer agrees with itself. A dialect is a property of the delimiter and quoting, never of the line terminator, so the sample is now newline-normalised before sniffing; the parsed text is untouched. **The falsifying input is pinned as an `@example`**: reverting the fix left the file green on a fresh seed, because generation found that input once and will not reliably find it again. With the pin, reverting fails on 3.12 and passes on 3.14, which is honest — the stdlib defect does not exist there)

- [ ] TASK-0285: Decide whether the assembled retrieved-context budget should be configurable, and whether 4,000 tokens is the right default. Surfaced by TASK-0247: having withdrawn the claim that ~2,500 tokens is a *chunk-size* ceiling and re-established it as a bound on **total retrieved context**, the repository now documents a cliff that its own shipped default runs ~1.6× past. `DEFAULT_CONTEXT_BUDGET_TOKENS = 4000` in `services/generation.py` is a constructor parameter with no `config.yaml` key reaching it, so an operator who reads the troubleshooting guidance and goes looking for the setting finds nothing — scope.md and troubleshooting.md now say so outright rather than implying a lever that does not exist. Two separable questions: expose it as a setting (cheap, and the docs already describe the behaviour), and whether the default should move toward R3's figure (not cheap — R3 is a single preliminary preprint on one setup, so changing a shipped default on it would be the same unbacked-number mistake in the other direction, and it wants the eval harness, not a citation)
- [x] TASK-0286: Gate the documents that assert what is *not* built, the way TASK-0255 ✅ gates internals.md's path map — ✅ 2026-11-02 (`scripts/check_claims.py` pairs every denial phrase with the task ids in its own scope and fails when an id is ticked, with two vacuity guards: a scan that read no documents, and a scan that matched no denial phrasing at all. Table rows resolve **cell-first** — a denial is paired with its own cell's id and only borrows the row's when its cell cites none — which is what caught the live false negative in release-readiness.md, a cell calling the cost governor non-existent beside a cell naming the ticked TASK-0242. Now wired into CI's `quality` job alongside the other doc gates; it had been committed but unwired, and a gate that is not in CI is not a gate. It immediately proved itself: landing the reindex chaos suite would have left four documents asserting it was unwritten, and the gate went red on them before TASK-0241 could be ticked)
- [x] TASK-0287: **The prompt was built from the wrong chunks.** `services/generation.py` assembled the prompt with `retrieved.chunks[: context.used]` — a slice by POSITION — while `assemble_context` packs SELECTIVELY, skipping blanks, near-duplicates and over-budget chunks and continuing past each one. `used` is a count, not an index, so the two agree only when nothing was skipped. Whenever anything was, the model was shown text that assembly had rejected — including text dropped for exceeding the context budget — while `citations` described what was actually packed. For a RAG library that is not cosmetic: faithfulness scoring and D5 grounded-or-refuse both grade an answer against citations the model may never have seen, so the guarantee they exist to provide was unsound in exactly the cases where it mattered. Present since `ceae504`, the original generation service. Fixed by returning the packed texts from assembly and building the prompt from those — ✅ 2026-10-28 (found only because TASK-0285 made the budget configurable and an adversarial reviewer checked whether the new setting actually bounded the prompt; it did not, and the reason turned out to predate the setting. Two tests pin it — the packed list against a positional slice, and that `packed` and `citations` describe the same chunks in the same order — and both go red against the old behaviour)
- [x] TASK-0288: `test_the_breaker_admits_one_probe_once_its_reset_timeout_passes` was flaky on Windows CI: the same commit failed one run and passed the next, asserting `HALF_OPEN` and getting `OPEN`. The test slept exactly `reset_timeout_ms` and then asserted the transition, which tests the clock rather than the breaker — asyncio fires a timer up to one `clock_resolution` **early** by design, and that resolution is ~15.6 ms on a Windows runner, the same coarse clock behind TASK-0262. The sleep now clears the resolution by a stated margin — ✅ 2026-10-28 (**not reproduced locally**: eight runs without the margin all passed on this machine, whose timer resolution is evidently finer than the runner's. The fix is reasoned from asyncio's documented early-firing behaviour and cannot make the assertion less likely to hold; CI is what actually judges it, and that is recorded here rather than claimed as verified)

Nothing here runs a real cross-encoder and no figure recorded is a measurement)

Its tests drive an in-memory double, which proves which commands are issued, that INCR and EXPIRE share one transaction, how keys are composed, and what an outage does — and cannot prove that redis-py and a real server agree with the double about any of it. Needs an integration test against a real server, which needs a hosted Redis this project does not currently have. `security.md` marks the backend unproven in production until then. **Narrowed 2026-11-04, not closed**: `tests/integration/test_redis_rate_limit.py` (7 cases) now drives the shipped `redis.asyncio.Redis` against fakeredis, which replaces the socket and nothing above it — so the command encoding, the `MULTI`/`EXEC` round trip, the reply parsing, and `INCR`/`EXPIRE`/expiry eviction are performed by a client and a server implementation rather than by this repository's idea of them, and every assertion reads state back out of that server. `fakeredis` is dev-only and deliberately gets no extra of its own: it is a test server, never something a deployment can install by accident. **What is still unproven is most of why this task exists** — no real Redis release is exercised, nothing crosses a socket so timeouts, resets, partitions, failover and pool exhaustion remain untested and the fail-open path is still reachable only by injecting an error, and the two clients sharing one ceiling share a Python object inside one interpreter, which is not the cross-process sharing the feature exists for. Still needs a hosted Redis.
- [x] TASK-0291: **`VectorDBAdapter.iterate_points` could not see `Point.sparse`, so sparse vectors were structurally unexportable** — ✅ 2026-11-04 (**the adapter layer only; the archive still does not carry the leg**). All three adapters now populate `Point.sparse` when `with_vectors` is asked for, in one neutral shape: `term_index` ids paired with the saturated term frequencies stored at ingest, ascending by term so a walk is reproducible across backends. IDF stays unstored by design (ADR-0007), so a carried vector rescores against whatever corpus receives it — correct, not a loss — and `k1`/`b` travel baked into the weights rather than being re-applied by the reader, which is the fidelity gap the recompute-on-import path has today. **Chroma's `fr.t.<index>` metadata is returned**: the representation is ours rather than a vendor's, but it is the only thing `_search_sparse`/`_rank` score a keyword query with, and refusing it would have made Chroma the one backend whose archives silently lose the leg. Four shared contract cases, including one that writes a walked collection into a *second* collection and requires the keyword ranking **and the float scores** to match — fidelity is the retrieval result, not the bytes, and a uniform-scale mutation proves that assertion is not vacuous. 18 mutations, 18 red, one restoring the exact pre-change behaviour. **Verified for real against Chroma only** (55/55, embedded). **Qdrant and pgvector are proven by unit tests against fakes and nothing else**: all four contract cases skipped for both (B7.2), the pgvector posting SQL has never reached a server, and it is unverified on a live Qdrant that `scroll(with_vectors=True)` returns a `models.SparseVector` under the `bm25` key — **in particular over gRPC**, which the agent named as the highest-risk assumption in the change. For TASK-0182's review: `with_vectors` is now overloaded to mean both legs, chosen over a separate `with_sparse` because Qdrant cannot return sparse without it, at the cost that a caller wanting only postings also pays for dense vectors)

- [x] TASK-0293: **`import_archive` materialised every member in memory** while export streamed — ✅ 2026-11-04 (both sides now stream: each member is copied into a private 0700 staging directory in 256 KiB blocks, hashed as it lands and charged block by block against the running budget, and every later pass reads those staged files a line at a time. **Peak memory is flat in archive size** — 1.55 MB for an 18 MB archive, and 4.70 MB vs 4.76 MB for 18 MB vs 36 MB at 1 MiB blocks. **Two defects found on the way.** First, the bomb ceiling added 2026-11-03 was *itself a memory bomb*: spending the budget with one `read(remaining + 1)` asked the allocator for the whole remaining 4 GiB on the first member — measured 4.33 GB peak while reading an 18 MB archive, so the guard against exhausting memory exhausted memory. Second, the first streaming draft leaked a staging directory on every mid-stream refusal, because the traceback kept the suspended row iterator and its open handle alive and Windows will not delete an open file — found as **61 abandoned directories each holding one `vectors.jsonl`**; the reader now owns its handles and closes them before cleanup. **Verify-before-write is intact** but its meaning narrowed, and this is stated rather than glossed: nothing reaches the *collection* before all four checks pass, yet unauthenticated bytes do land on local disk first, bounded by the same budget, refused on any member that is not a plain file at the archive root — the traversal check is now load-bearing rather than belt-and-braces — and removed on every failure path. Vectors are read in lockstep with the chunk stream; a shuffled archive stays legal and falls back to an offset index. 10 new tests, 25 mutations all red, including a tracemalloc-bounded test that fails against the old reader with 'held 4327722000 bytes while reading a 18082113 byte archive'. **Two mutations initially survived and were reported, not hidden** — both were masked by the TemporaryDirectory finalizer, and sharper mutations of the same guarantee replaced them. **Proven against a fake adapter only**: the new live assertion skips with the other eight D11 tests for want of Docker, so nothing has streamed into or out of a real backend and no corpus larger than RAM has been imported. **Leaves open**: memory is O(rows) not O(1), and `api/admin.py` and `cli/commands/portability.py` still rely on garbage collection rather than `with open_archive(...)`)
- [x] TASK-0294: **The SSRF guard did not stop DNS rebinding** — ✅ 2026-11-04 (the verified address is now pinned into the connection, so there is no second resolution for a TTL-0 record to poison. The hard part was not breaking TLS: the real hostname travels as the `sni_hostname` extension, which httpcore passes as `server_hostname`, so the certificate is still checked against the **name** and not the address; `Host` is sent explicitly from the original netloc so virtual hosting survives; `raw_host` is used so an IDN is punycode, matching what httpcore would have derived. **Keepalive is disabled for these fetches** because httpcore pools by origin and a pinned origin is `(scheme, IP, port)` — two hostnames sharing an address would otherwise reuse a TLS session established and verified for the first. A relative `Location` is joined against the caller's URL, not the pinned response URL, which would lose the name the guard and the certificate check both need. The rebinding test needs no DNS: a resolver answers public first and link-local second, and the test asserts it was consulted exactly once. **Not proven by test: no live TLS handshake runs against a pinned address** — the certificate-verification claim rests on reading httpcore's `_connect`, and that limit is recorded in security.md §5.1 rather than implied away)
- [ ] TASK-0295: (maintainer decision) **A key entry that omits its tenant becomes an operator credential** able to act for any tenant. That is deliberate and documented, but the broader capability is what an operator gets by leaving a field off — the opposite of how `required_scope` treats omission, which fails closed by design. Startup now logs a WARNING naming each unbound key (2026-11-03), which makes it visible without breaking anyone. Requiring an explicit marker (say `*`) would be the secure shape and would invalidate every existing key file; that trade is the decision. **Confirmed still live after TASK-0327 (2026-11-08)**: `k:query` and `k:query:` both yield `tenant=None`, pinned by `test_a_trailing_colon_after_the_scopes_leaves_the_key_unbound`. Unlike the scope field, this grant is documented, warned about at startup and has no non-breaking replacement, which is the decision. Note that `*` now exists in this grammar and is the obvious any-tenant marker, but `k:query:*` currently means the literal tenant id `*`, so adopting it in field three is a reservation that must be made consciously.
- [x] TASK-0296: **`POST /v1/admin/export` wrote to a caller-supplied path and `POST /v1/admin/import` read one**, with no confinement — ✅ 2026-11-04 (both now pass through `guard_archive_path`, confined by a single `security.allowed_archive_roots`. One list for both verbs, because export writes and import read the same artifact in the same directory, so two lists would be set identically by every operator and could only diverge by mistake. Same shape as the ingestion guard it sits beside: resolve before comparing, so `..` collapses and a symlink inside a root cannot point out of it, and an empty list is **refused** once `security.auth` is on — forgetting the setting breaks a served deployment loudly rather than exposing it quietly. Refusals are RFC 9457 `422` naming the setting. 12 tests, each mutation-verified in both directions. The CLI path is deliberately unguarded: it runs as the operator)
- [ ] TASK-0297: **Build a golden set large enough to price reranking.** BENCH-0003 (2026-11-03, `scripts/measure_rerank.py`) measured the rerank trade across `rerank: false`, `cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2` (87.56 MB) and the shipped `BAAI/bge-reranker-v2-m3` (2.14 GB), reusing `core/evals.evaluate` so the numbers sit beside the committed baselines. It is **input to TASK-0175, not a resolution of it**, and its most useful result is a negative one: on the committed golden sets the quality gain from reranking is **inside its own noise** (policies/768t: MRR +0.0938 ± 0.0504, nDCG +0.0692 ± 0.0372 over 16 queries — 1.86σ, below the 2σ bar), `handbook` is **fully saturated** (every configuration scores 1.0 on all three metrics), recall@5 is 1.0 in every configuration measured, and **no measured difference separates the 2.2 GB default from the 90 MB alternative** — they tie on two comparisons and the small model is marginally ahead on the third (MRR 0.9688 vs 0.9583 at 128-token chunks). A 12-to-24 chunk corpus is not a retrieval problem a cross-encoder is needed for. **Not blocked on TASK-0084's isolated hardware**: the constraint is dataset size, so no machine fixes it, and TASK-0175 cannot be decided on quality grounds until a golden set one or two orders of magnitude larger exists over a corpus where recall@5 is not already saturated.
- [x] TASK-0298: **Correct the “over 400 s to load” figure** carried by TASK-0174 ✅ and quoted by TASK-0175, `blockers.md` B6, and the `# CRITICAL:` comment in `core/rerank/cross_encoder.py`. BENCH-0003 measured it: the 2.2 GB reranker **downloads** in 357 s and then **loads in 8.3–9.3 s** from a populated cache — about one second more than the 90 MB model's 7.3–7.7 s. The first-run cost is a one-time 2.14 GB fetch, not a per-process load, and those sites currently read as though every start pays minutes. The real recurring cost is elsewhere and larger: **17–25×** the small model's per-query rerank latency, which at the shipped `retrieval.rerank_top_n: 100` is **55.7–86.9 s per query** — CPU-only (`torch 2.13.0+cpu`; no GPU was measured and a GPU would change this by orders of magnitude). TASK-0175's trade should be re-stated against these numbers before it is decided. — ✅ 2026-11-04 (all three editable sites now carry BENCH-0003's numbers: TASK-0175's entry, `blockers.md` B6, and the `# CRITICAL:` comment in `core/rerank/cross_encoder.py`, each separating the one-time 357 s **download** from the 8.3–9.3 s **load** and naming the recurring 17–25× per-query cost that the old figure hid entirely. **TASK-0174 ✅ keeps the wrong number**: ticked entries are append-only and frozen, so the correction lives here and at the three live sites rather than being edited into history)
- [ ] TASK-0299: **Re-check the four accepted `chromadb` advisories and delete the ignores the moment a fix exists.** Landing the Chroma adapter (TASK-0049) pulled in `chromadb` 1.5.9, which carries PYSEC-2026-311, -3813, -3814 and -3815 — two code injections through `trust_remote_code` (one pre-authentication) and two cross-tenant authorization failures. **1.5.9 is the latest release, so there is no version to upgrade to.** All four are in Chroma's HTTP server, which fasterRag never starts: the adapter uses an embedded `PersistentClient`, serves no API, never constructs `SimpleRBACAuthorizationProvider`, and passes vectors itself rather than configuring a Chroma-side embedding function — the path `trust_remote_code` travels. That reasoning is ours, read off the shipped code, not a vendor statement, and it holds only for embedded use. Accepted on maintainer authorization 2026-11-04 as four named `--ignore-vuln` entries in the supply-chain job, justified inline; verified exactly scoped — the audit reports 5 findings without them and `No known vulnerabilities found, 5 ignored` with them, so every other advisory still blocks the build. Revisit whenever chromadb publishes a release, and delete all four ignores if the Chroma extra is ever dropped.
- [ ] TASK-0300: **Close the 63 surviving mutants the first mutation run found.** Measured 2026-11-04 over four of six targets (134 mutants, 71 killed). Each survivor is a line that can be changed without any test noticing. Sharpest first: **`core/identity.py:116`**, where *four independent mutations of one predicate all survive*, so that condition is effectively untested — and it is the module behind `index.lock` drift detection and D1's zero-false-negatives bound; **`core/breaker.py:68/69`**, where the shipped `failure_threshold=5` and `reset_timeout_ms=30_000` defaults are never exercised because every test constructs the breaker explicitly; **`core/breaker.py:82`** and **`core/rate_limit.py:138`**, both `>=`→`>` boundaries — nothing sits exactly on the reset timeout, and nothing pins the window-eviction boundary (**correction, 2026-11-04**: line 138 is the *eviction* test, not the limit comparison; `rate_limit.py:141`'s `len(window) >= self.limit` was already killed in the first run, so 'allow N vs N+1' was never the undefended part); the whole retry-after arithmetic in both the breaker and the limiter, unasserted; and **`services/regression.py:402`** (`<=`→`<`), the tolerance boundary the D7 gate CI now blocks on. Full list in the agent report and reproducible with `python scripts/mutation_run.py`. **⚠ Every figure in this entry was measured with the unfixed runner (TASK-0303 ✅ 2026-11-07) and the first-run split is not trustworthy.** Hole (a) meant tests were re-read live from the working tree on every mutant, and these slices were editing the very test files they were scoring, so a verdict could flip for reasons unrelated to the mutation — which is also the unexplained 13-vs-14 result at `breaker.py:43`. The direction is almost certainly right (the gaps are real and the tests written against them are real), but the counts want re-deriving on the fixed runner. **`breaker` is the one target re-verified**: 32 of 34 killed, same two survivors, byte-identical report. A narrower caveat applies to the 2026-11-07 slices below: they ran private copies of the then-unfixed runner, but each owned a disjoint set of test files that no other agent touched, so hole (a) could not reach them — hole (b), a stale `.pyc` masking a length-preserving mutant, still could. **All six targets are now measured and five are closed (2026-11-04).** `core/identity` 13→2, `core/breaker` 13→2, `services/regression` 14→2, `core/rate_limit` 22→3 and `core/retrieval` 18→4 survivors; every remaining survivor in those five carries a written equivalence proof so the next run does not re-litigate it. **`core/chunking` was measured for the first time and is the one still open**: 234 mutants generated, a seeded sample of 120 executed (`--only chunking --budget 120 --seed 0`), 56 killed / 64 survived at baseline and **94 killed / 26 survived** after 33 new tests — 46.7% → 78.3% **on the sample, with no score claimed for the 114 unsampled mutants**. **The sharpest finding of the whole exercise was in `core/retrieval`**: the entire *magnitude* half of BM25 was unpinned. `frequency * (k1 + 1)` survived becoming `frequency / (k1 + 1)` and `frequency * (k1 + 0)`, and `AVERAGE_DOCUMENT_LENGTH` survived 256→257, against 1,356 lines of tests including 17 Hypothesis properties — the suite asserted *shape* (ordering, bounds, monotonicity) and never a number, so any scoring function with the same ordering would have passed. RRF was clean because it already had a formula oracle; BM25 has one now. The chunking gaps were the same class: `EstimatingTokenCounter.count` could be made to return 0 for every string, the overlap could be switched off outright, the recursive and layout packing anchors could be moved, and the shipped `768`/`64` defaults were never exercised because every test passed explicit sizes — all with 122 tests green. Still open in chunking: the three `pooling.py:103` centrality mutants (killing them needs a fake network whose output encodes a token's position *within* its window — until then, on a document longer than the model's context a token can silently take its vector from the window where it is least contextual, which is the entire point of windowing), the `semantic.py:96/97` and `layout.py:36` defaults, and `models.py:180` (`merged[-1]` → `merged[0]`), where no blank-segment input could be constructed and **no equivalence is claimed**.
- [x] TASK-0301: `api/admin.py` and `cli/commands/portability.py` call `open_archive` without `close()`, relying on the `TemporaryDirectory` finalizer to release the staging directory at garbage collection. Correct today but non-deterministic; found 2026-11-04 while streaming the import (TASK-0293 ✅), which also showed how a retained traceback can keep a handle alive long enough for Windows to refuse the delete. `with open_archive(...) as reader:` in both places makes the release deterministic — a one-line change in each, in a scope that owns those files. — ✅ 2026-11-04 (both call sites now scope the reader: `with open_archive(...) as reader:` in `api/admin.py`, and `with reader:` in the CLI, where the reader is opened before the block because its own construction failure is reported as a different exit code. Release is now deterministic rather than whenever the collector runs — which on Windows is the difference between a staging directory being removed and a later run tripping over it, as TASK-0293 found the hard way with 61 abandoned directories)
- [x] TASK-0302: The export side still drops the keyword leg. TASK-0291 ✅ made `iterate_points` return `Point.sparse` on all three adapters, but `services/archive.py` and `vectors.jsonl` were deliberately left unchanged, so a `.fragx` still carries dense vectors only and import still recomputes BM25 under the *target's* `k1`/`b` rather than the weights the archive was written with. Closing this is the other half of D11 fidelity. Note that `with_vectors` now means both legs, which TASK-0182's review of `iterate_points` should settle before it hardens. — ✅ 2026-11-04 (`vectors.jsonl` rows now carry an optional `sparse` object and the manifest declares `includes_sparse`, so an archive transplants the weights the corpus was indexed with instead of recomputing BM25 under the target's `k1`/`b`. **The format version bump is conditional**: an export with no postings still declares `1.0.0` and writes byte-for-byte what it wrote before, so nothing is stranded over a feature it does not use. **The bump exists precisely to cause a refusal** — a reader older than this change validates only the major and ignores unknown fields, so without it such a reader would import a hybrid archive and silently rebuild the keyword leg with different parameters, producing a collection that ranks differently from its source with nothing reporting it. For the same reason the leg extends `vectors.jsonl` rather than adding a `sparse.jsonl`: the existing reader stages unknown members silently, so a new member would have been dropped without a word by every build ever shipped. Migration note in `CHANGELOG.md` with the 2.x → 1.x conversion procedure, as the spec's own compatibility rule requires of a major bump. **Proven against fake adapters only** — the assertion that matters, that a restored collection's keyword *ranking and float scores* match the source's with the source indexed at a deliberately non-default `k1=0.4, b=0.1` so a recompute could not match, is `tests/integration/test_archive_round_trip.py`, and all twelve tests there skip for want of Docker. No archive has carried a keyword leg through a real vector database)
- [x] TASK-0303: **`scripts/mutation_run.py` is isolated on only one side, and the second hole is a silent false *pass*.** Found 2026-11-04 while closing the breaker slice of TASK-0300. (a) The runner snapshots `src/` into a workspace but invokes pytest with `cwd=REPOSITORY_ROOT` and **relative** test paths, so the tests are re-read live from the working tree on every one of a target's invocations — any edit landing mid-run changes the verdict of every mutant executed afterwards. Demonstrated by flipping `core/breaker.py:35` from survived to killed with `src/` byte-identical, purely by editing the test file between runs; this also explains the previously unexplained 13-vs-14 result at `breaker.py:43`. Fix by copying the named test paths into the workspace too. (b) `copytree` carries `src/`'s `__pycache__` into the workspace, and CPython validates a cached `.pyc` on `(int source mtime seconds, source size)` alone — so a **byte-length-preserving** mutant such as `'0'->'1'` survives silently whenever the mtime collides, because the mutation is never executed. Proved by rolling the mtime back (exit 0, survived) against a fresh mtime (exit 1, killed). Not reachable at the current ~3 s/mutant cadence on a ~1 µs-granularity volume, but it reports a mutant as killed-or-survived without running it, which is exactly the T12 vacuity class this runner exists to detect. Fix with `ignore=shutil.ignore_patterns("__pycache__")` or `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1` in the child environment. **Until (a) is fixed, any mutation run taken while another agent is editing that target's tests is unreliable.** — ✅ 2026-11-07 (both closed. `run()` now calls `stage_suite`, which copies the **whole** `tests/` tree plus `pyproject.toml` into the workspace, and `staged_tests` maps each target's named paths onto the copies, **refusing a path the snapshot lacks rather than falling back to the live tree**. The whole tree, not only the named paths: a first attempt that staged just the named files plus their `__init__.py`/`conftest.py` chain turned the `regression` baseline red, because `test_comparability.py:33` reaches `tests/eval/datasets` through `__file__` — measured cost of the full copy is 0.8–1.3 s per run over 2.1 MB / 236 files. Both `copytree` calls now pass `ignore=shutil.ignore_patterns("__pycache__")` and the child carries `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1`; **both halves are needed**, since the ignore stops `src/`'s cache riding in while the env var stops mutant *N*'s `.pyc` outliving it into mutant *N+1*, which is the same mtime-seconds collision one layer in. CLI and JSON shape unchanged. **`--only breaker --seed 0` is unmoved**: 34/34 run, 32 killed, 2 survived, the same two survivors, and the two JSON reports are byte-identical except `duration_s` — so the fix changed isolation without changing a verdict. Staged baselines verified green for all six targets. Five regression tests, **each proved red against a kept copy of the unfixed runner**: the mid-run edit scenario returned killed=1/survived=0 before and survived=1 after; the stale-cache scenario returned survived=1 before — while reporting `executed=1` for a mutant that never ran, the vacuity in its purest form — and killed=1 after; the workspace held 2 `.pyc` files before and none after. **Two things were not verified.** No deterministic *timestamp* collision could be built from a test, because the runner writes the mutant itself so pinning `int(mtime)` is a race; the planted `.pyc` therefore uses `UNCHECKED_HASH`, which is the same shadowing defect with a deterministic trigger, and the timestamp half is covered only structurally by asserting no bytecode survives in the workspace. And no wall-clock before/after is quotable — measured old 48 s, new 131 s, new 87 s, old 203 s in that order, which is nine agents sharing a box rather than a result; the author deleted the "48 s → 87 s" line they had first written rather than publish it. The real cost is ~1 s of copying per run plus a full recompile per mutant, unmeasured on quiet hardware. One behaviour change: a `--targets-file` naming a test path outside `tests/` is now refused as `not-run` instead of silently running against the live tree)
- [ ] TASK-0304: **`normalise_source` treats any single Unicode alphabetic character as a Windows drive letter, so two distinct POSIX files can mint one `document_id` and the second is silently dropped as a duplicate.** `core/identity.py:116` guards on `drive.isalpha()`, which is Unicode-wide, while a Windows drive is exactly `[A-Za-z]`. Reproduced 2026-11-04: `normalise_source('É:/Docs/A.PDF')` and `normalise_source('é:/docs/a.pdf')` return the same string, and on POSIX a directory may legally be named `É:` — so the two files collide and dedup discards one. Data loss, not a cosmetic issue, though it needs an unusual corpus to hit. The existing suite cannot see it: `test_distinct_sources_get_distinct_documents` assumes `normalise_source(a) != normalise_source(b)` for distinct inputs. **Narrowing the predicate changes minted ids**, so it is not a free fix — it ships with an `IDENTITY_VERSION` bump at the next scheme change, which is why it was found and left rather than patched during TASK-0300's identity slice. The related `C:/Reports/` ambiguity on Linux is inherent to the heuristic and documented; this one is not.
- [ ] TASK-0305: **Qdrant — the reference adapter — accepts a `create_collection` whose spec asks for a keyword leg the live collection does not have, where pgvector and Chroma both refuse.** `adapters/vectordb/qdrant.py:419` `_require_compatible` checks named-dense presence, dimensions and distance but never `spec.sparse`, so creation passes quietly and the ingest job instead dies at the first `upsert` with `EmbedError` from `_vector_for` — a confusing failure, far from its cause. `pgvector.py:702` and `chroma.py:482` raise `CONFLICT` at creation naming the real problem. Found 2026-11-04 while building TASK-0119's detection. One `if spec.sparse and not sparse_vectors: raise CONFLICT` brings the reference adapter in line with the other two; it is an adapter boundary change, so it wants its own authorization. **Read from `_require_compatible`, not observed against a live Qdrant.**
- [x] TASK-0306: **No gate sees a `TASK-` id inside source code**, so a docstring can cite a task that is ticked, superseded or nonexistent and every check stays green. `check_claims.py` and `check_doc_truth.py` both scan `docs/` only — verified 2026-11-04 — while `src/` and `tests/` carry task ids in docstrings and in `# CRITICAL:` / `# TODO:` / `# BLOCKED:` markers, which is exactly where a reader looks for why a thing is the way it is. Found when `core/rate_limit.py:45` and `tests/unit/core/test_rate_limit.py` were left citing TASK-0216 for the untested-against-a-real-server claim after the ledger moved that gap to TASK-0290; both gates passed with the pointers wrong. This is the same defect class the FMEA parity gate (TASK-0289 ✅) closed for `failure-modes.md`, one layer down. Minimum useful version: every `TASK-####` appearing anywhere in `src/` or `tests/` must exist in todo.md, and a marker citing a **ticked** id must be reviewed — a `# TODO:` pointing at finished work is either stale or the work was not finished. Needs a vacuity guard like its siblings: a scan matching zero ids must fail rather than report a clean tree. — ✅ 2026-11-07 (`scripts/check_task_ids.py` + 20 tests, wired into ci.yml's quality job as "Task ids cited in code resolve"; CI-only, not pre-commit. Two rules: an undefined id anywhere in a comment or docstring under `src/`/`tests/`/`scripts/` fails, and a `# TODO:`/`# BLOCKED:` marker — including its continuation block — citing a **ticked** id fails. **Failure, not warning, decided deliberately**: the condition is mechanically decidable rather than a judgement call, every fix is local (repoint, delete, or untick), a warning in a green log is read by nobody — which is how the original drift survived — and it fires on 1 of 140 real citations. A **docstring** or a `# CRITICAL:` block citing a ticked id stays green: that is history correctly attributed, and invariant comments routinely name the task that established them. **Scope is comments and docstrings only, not arbitrary string literals**, and the reason is evidence not timidity: `tests/unit/scripts/test_check_blockers.py:27` feeds `TASK-9999` to the blockers gate as fixture data on purpose, so a literal-scanning gate would fail correct code on its first run. The cost — an id in a dict literal or a skip reason is invisible — is stated in the module docstring rather than hidden. Three vacuity guards, all tested: zero citations matched, zero `.py` files found, and a ledger defining zero entries each fail. Measured on the real tree: 283 ledger entries / 239 ticked, 140 citations resolved across 59 distinct ids, 2 inside pending markers. **Every cited id resolves — no dangling reference exists** — and the single failure was real: `tests/eval/test_live_providers.py:167`, now repointed at TASK-0312)
- [ ] TASK-0307: **`LayoutChunker` never packs, so any block larger than `chunk_size` shatters into one chunk per separator.** Found 2026-11-04 by the chunking slice of TASK-0300. `RecursiveChunker.segments` wraps its `split_span` result in `recursive.pack`; `layout.py:57` (no-blocks fallback) and `layout.py:103` (oversized block) both call `split_span` directly, and `split_span` returns the *finest* tiling that fits — one piece per separator occurrence. Measured with the shipped defaults (`chunk_size: 768`, `overlap: 64`) on a 6,207-character Markdown document of one heading plus one paragraph: **121 layout chunks averaging ~50 characters of new text, against 3 from the recursive baseline, and 36,139 characters of chunk text for a 6,207-character document (5.9×)**; a 3,000-word single-line plaintext document yields 3,000 six-character chunks. **All five documented invariants pass throughout** — the tiling stays gapless, offsets stay exact, overlap stays within budget and no chunk exceeds its token limit — the chunks are simply tiny and ~98% duplicated by the 256-character reach-back, which is precisely why no existing test saw it. Because `core/identity` derives chunk ids from boundaries, `strategy: layout` currently mints roughly 40× the ids it should. Fix validated in an isolated copy of `src/`: import `pack` and wrap both `split_span` calls in `pack(..., self._limit)` — 121 chunks → 4, emitted text 36,139 → 6,727 characters, chunking suite and `test_identity.py` green. **It is a boundary change and re-ids every corpus chunked with `strategy: layout`, so it needs maintainer authorization rather than a quiet patch.** Guarded today by `tests/unit/core/chunking/test_boundaries.py::test_layout_packs_an_oversized_block_back_up_to_the_chunk_size`, `xfail(strict=True)` so it fails loudly the moment someone fixes it and forgets the marker — delete the marker with the fix. Closing this also unblocks the `layout.py:36` default-`chunk_size` survivor, which cannot be pinned while the oversized path is wrong.

Twenty are corrected in the same commit series; what stays open is the reason they accumulated. **Three structural gaps, each verified by running `check_claims.py` green over a tree containing every finding.** (a) The gate never reads `README.md` or `CLAUDE.md` — it iterates `docs/` only — so the literal sentence it was built to stop (“`--watch` is declared but not yet implemented”, matching its own `not[\s-]+yet[\s-]+implemented` pattern) survived on the page PyPI will show, for a flag that shipped. No regex fixes this — it needs denials to cite an id covering exactly the thing denied, or a per-item sub-ledger. (c) **The gate looks only for denials, and the overclaims are the dangerous half.** “Verified against a live Qdrant”, “chunk/metadata loss = 0” for a property measured lossy, a two-entry ledger that holds three, a release score stale by one re-derivation, “trunk-based, no feature branches” after the PR instruction superseded it — all invisible by construction. The project caught the denial class three times and built a gate; it has been bitten by the affirmative class at least four times and built nothing. Also recorded: the policy names a *periodic human grep* as its enforcement, so enforcement is exactly as reliable as somebody remembering, and 24 defects accumulated in one gap. **Closed, and the previous sweep's own corrections decayed in under 48 hours.** Of the 2026-11-07/08 corpus, ~21 hold, **2 classes regressed** (README's release score went stale the day after correction; the adapter set went stale again on the very documents that had just been corrected, as Milvus landed 11-07 and Weaviate 11-08), and **3 were never made** — `CONTRIBUTING.md` still carried "Trunk-based development… No feature branches" against CLAUDE.md's 2026-10-28 PR instruction, `rebuild-playbook.md` the same, and `reliability.md` said "Feature branches **only**", so two documents in one repository directly contradicted each other. **35 new statement-sites across 7 classes; 33 fixed, 2 filed.** The sharpest: `CLAUDE.md:9` said S14 was open while both its tasks are ticked and `observability/dashboard.py` ships, so the always-loaded instruction file contradicted README and quickstart; `docs/structure.md` violated the contract stated in **its own second line** in nine places (six `(pending)` markers on shipped modules, three listed paths that do not exist); `testing-strategy.md` counted a **skip as a pass** ("contract suite green for all in-repo adapters"). **The gate-gap finding is the deliverable**: eight of the nine doc gates never ask the tree a question and the ninth asks the ledger as a proxy for it — they all answer "is this document consistent with another document?" while all 24 defects were "is this document consistent with the code?". A consistency gate over a closed set of documents produces false confidence **in proportion to how well it works**, because the green tick reads as "the docs are true". **Two of seven classes are mechanically decidable and are now gated** by `scripts/check_unbuilt_claims.py` (CI quality job, "Denials match the tree, not just the ledger"), which parses `_BUILTIN_ADAPTERS`/`_PLANNED_ADAPTERS` out of the three factories — a mapping that cannot rot, because an adapter does not load without it — and asks whether a denial clause names something registered. **The rest need a human, and saying so is the finding**: "verified against a live Qdrant" is ordinary English. 4 adapter findings over 398 clause-mentions and 9 structure findings over a 90-entry parse, **0 false positives**; the one the first version produced is why a provider introduced by an affirmation within 28 characters is not counted, and why clauses split on `;`/`:`/dash and never on `" and "`, which would break the enumeration the gate exists for. Four vacuity guards, each tested, including one that fires when **nothing is planned** — with no planned provider every denial is automatically false and the rule stops discriminating. Ratchet by path, shipped empty and pinned empty. Independently re-verified by the orchestrator: the gate fires on the exact `docs/scope.md` sentence that was wrong on 2026-11-07, and would have caught Chroma on 11-03 and Milvus on 11-07. Also landed: `tests/unit/docs/test_cli_flag_parity.py`, salvaged under TASK-0284, which arrived 6/9 green — the six passing were its own vacuity guards — and whose three failures were **nine real defects in `cli-reference.md`**, including `autopilot run --golden-set` documented and nonexistent. — ✅ 2026-11-09

Its method was validated against its own stated date — `src/` at the last commit before 2026-10-30 is 27,477 lines, i.e. the quoted “27.5k” exactly. **Both ledger-count traps are pinned by tests**: the naive `### BENCH-` count is 4 and the answer is 3 because one is a template, and `^## Ledger` had to be anchored to end-of-line or it matches `## Ledger rules` and returns a ledger of zero. `test_superlative_scan.py` makes the policy's “periodic human grep” run on every push: 30 raw matches, 19 exempt as quoted mentions, 9 as goals, 1 allowlisted, **0 findings**. Precision was measured rather than hoped — `cheapest` and `quickest` each fired once and both were ordinary English, so `cheapest` was dropped and `quickest` allowlisted in public; bare `best` (2 of 5 ordinary English) and bare `most <word>` (~27 of 30) are **deliberately not blocked**, because a gate firing on “at most four lines” gets disabled and takes the real hits with it. - [x] TASK-0312: **`CohereEmbedder` and the Ollama embedder discard the token usage their providers report**, so `reports_token_usage=False` in `tests/eval/test_live_providers.py` pins absent accounting rather than asserting it. Cohere returns billed units in `response.meta` and `adapters/embeddings/cohere.py:98` returns only the vectors, dropping it. That matters beyond tidiness: the cost estimator and the runtime governor (TASK-0242 ✅) price these providers from locally-counted tokens rather than from what the provider says it billed, so a deployment on Cohere embeddings cannot reconcile its budget against an invoice. Surfaced 2026-11-07 by TASK-0306's new gate, which caught the `# TODO:` at `tests/eval/test_live_providers.py:164-167` citing TASK-0205 ✅ for a follow-up that was never filed — precisely the marker-on-finished-work defect the gate exists to find, and the first thing it found. **Closed.** Cohere now reports `meta.billed_units.input_tokens`; OpenAI returns `None` instead of a false `0` when a gateway omits `usage`, so an unreported response is no longer priced as free. `EmbeddingWorkerPool` prices every batch through `services.estimation.price_for` into `fasterrag_tokens_total{kind="embedding"}` / `fasterrag_cost_usd_total` / `fasterrag_unpriced_tokens_total` and records to the shared `CostGovernor` — embedding spend previously reached no cost metric at all, and `EmbeddingResult.total_tokens` was consumed by nothing but the cache. Counted and reported tokens will disagree (local is 4-chars-per-token; Cohere bills its own tokenizer), so the provider's figure is billed where there is one and the difference is published as `EmbedReport.token_drift` rather than silently resolved. **Ollama reports nothing on the route we call**: `OllamaEmbedder` uses the legacy `/api/embeddings`, whose documented body is a bare `{"embedding": [...]}`; `/api/embed` does return `prompt_eval_count` but changing route raises the minimum server version, so it is TASK-0359 rather than a quiet bump. The stale `# TODO:` citing ticked TASK-0205 is gone, and `LiveCheck.__post_init__` now **refuses** a `reports_token_usage=False` carrying no stated reason — the flag pinned the absence of accounting as if it were the design. **No live call was made**: the Cohere shape is from the vendor reference, and neither the `cohere` nor `ollama` SDK is installed here, so the fakes are unchecked against installed types. If the shape is wrong the live probe now fails rather than passing vacuously. — ✅ 2026-11-08

A second gap: `concurrency: cancel-in-progress` on `github.ref` cancelled 21 of 151 `ci.yml` runs (all 21 confirmed superseded within 10 minutes, none a disguised failure), leaving **21 commits on `main` with no completed verdict at all**, which CLAUDE.md's green-on-every-commit rule does not permit. Two halves, either order: (a) TASK-0098's required status check on `main`, which also stops the cancellations landing unverified commits; (b) a `fasterrag doctor`-style command, or a CI step, that reads the Actions API for the last conclusion of each of the three workflows on `main` and fails when any is red — a scheduled workflow nobody watches is exactly the gate this task exists to replace. **Half of it already exists, unadopted.** The TASK-0284 audit found `services/ci_status.py` (381 lines) plus `test_ci_status.py` (513 lines, 28 tests) in an abandoned worktree, **passing against today's `src/`**, with every `fasterrag` module it imports still present. Nothing on main reads the Actions API, so it is unsuperseded. It needs CLI wiring and a decision about where the check belongs (`fasterrag doctor`, a dedicated command, or a CI step). The other half, TASK-0098's branch protection, remains a maintainer action in GitHub settings. **Half (b) landed 2026-11-11: `fasterrag ci-status` plus a `branch-verdict` job in `ci.yml`. Half (a), TASK-0098's branch protection, is still a maintainer action in GitHub settings, so this entry stays open.** `services/ci_status.py` and its 28 tests were salvaged from the worktree TASK-0284 names and ran **45/45 green against today's `src/` before a line was changed**; what was built on top is the gate. **The listing is never filtered by trigger** — the defect is that the push run a human opens can be green while a scheduled run of the same commit is red, which is reproduced in the tests from this repository's own payloads. Four states exit non-zero: a red run, a cancelled one, a workflow with no completed run, and an unreadable API. The workflow set is **discovered** from `.github/workflows`, so a fourth workflow is gated the day it lands, and an empty set fails rather than passing. **Two vacuity shapes beyond the brief**: GitHub concludes a run `success` when every job in it was **skipped**, so `passed` now also requires at least one job that ran; and the report names every later step a failure caused to be skipped (thirteen on run 42522906097 — see TASK-0383). **`--exclude ci.yml` is required, not convenient**: a workflow gating on its own previous conclusion is deadlocked (the push that fixes the cause still reads the old red) or vacuous (the last completed run is always an ancestor), with nothing between.
- [x] TASK-0314: **The nightly benchmark has measured nothing for at least 83 days while reporting success every night.** The `measure` job in `nightly.yml` declares no `services:` block — verified 2026-11-07, the file contains zero of them, unlike `ci.yml`'s `eval-gate` which declares a Qdrant service — so `fasterrag benchmark --json` cannot work there by construction and dies with `error: [RETRIEVAL_FAILED] qdrant was unreachable during search`. **The failure is swallowed twice**, by `continue-on-error: true` and by `|| true` on the same step, leaving `artifacts/benchmark.json` empty; `scripts/compare_nightly.py` then correctly reports `[not-comparable] ... is not a readable benchmark document` for both the current and the previous night, and `nightly.yml` maps that verdict to `::warning::` and keeps the job green. **The script is honest and the workflow waves it through.** Measured: 15 of 15 sampled runs (every 7th from 2026-08-16 to 2026-11-01, plus 11-03 through 11-06) show the benchmark step failing and the job concluding success; `measure` has concluded success in **95 of 95 runs** since 2026-08-04. Nothing earlier is checkable — GitHub's 90-day log retention has expired the first two runs — but the step is unchanged since `074c650`, so it likely never worked; the claim is deliberately bounded at 83 days rather than extended to the introduction date. This is the T12 vacuity defect **in the one job whose purpose is to make the provable-claims policy infrastructure**. It falsifies two ticked entries: TASK-0246's "a night that compared nothing can never read as green" — it reads green every night, and that entry's own "Unverified: the first scheduled nightly is its first evidence" note is now answered in the negative — and TASK-0161's claim that the job uploads ledger-format benchmark artifacts, when it uploads an empty file. Fix: give `measure` the Qdrant service `eval-gate` already has, drop at least one of the two suppressions, and make verdict 2 fail the job the way `ci.yml:362` already makes the same verdict fail the eval gate. Then correct the closing notes on TASK-0246 and TASK-0161. — ✅ 2026-11-08 (the `measure` job now declares the Qdrant service `eval-gate` already had, `|| true` is gone from the benchmark step, and **verdict 2 now fails the job**, identical in consequence to `ci.yml`'s `not-comparable` branch — that asymmetry between two callers of one concept was the bug. `continue-on-error` stays, for the reason it was added, but can no longer launder a non-run into a green night. **The prescription in this entry was necessary and not sufficient**, which only reading the code revealed: `run_benchmark` defaults to `--suite all`, the *query* suite runs first and calls the configured LLM with CI's `ci-placeholder` key, and returns on failure — so ingest was never reached and no document was ever written, service or no service; ingest additionally refuses without `--sources`. The job now runs `--suite ingest --sources tests/eval/datasets/handbook/corpus` with `chunking.token_counter=estimate` pinned, because under `auto` the token count depends on the runner's tokenizer cache and lands in the `dataset` field that `compare_nightly` refuses to compare across — `auto` would have produced random red nights. Four tests, each **proved red against `git show HEAD:.github/workflows/nightly.yml`**. `compare_nightly.py` itself was already honest and its logic is unchanged; two of its docstrings were corrected, including one claiming the step runs under `|| true`. **The first night after this lands is expected to be red**, because its baseline is the previous night's empty document — that is the gate working. **Nothing has run on a GitHub runner**; all verification is local `yaml.safe_load` and `bash -n`)
- [x] TASK-0315: **The push-path secret scan reads one commit while its own comment claims it reads full history.** `ci.yml` is prefaced "Scans full history, not the diff. A secret committed and later removed is still in the history, and still compromised — checking only the tip would call it clean", and `security.yml` carries the same claim. Measured on the green HEAD run `41865567333`: `gitleaks detect ... --log-opts=-1` → `1 commits scanned`, `~3188 bytes`, no leaks. `fetch-depth: 0` is set and then discarded, because `gitleaks-action@v2` passes `--log-opts=-1` on `push` events; on the `schedule` trigger the same action scans **290 commits, 6.26 MB**. **The two disagree on the same SHA**, which is how the gap surfaced: the scheduled scan failed on 2026-11-05 and 11-06 over a string the push scan of that identical commit called clean. That specific hit was a false positive — a rate-limiter identity fixture shaped like a Stripe key, not a credential — and is **fixed 2026-11-07** by renaming the value rather than adding a `.gitleaksignore`, since a suppression file would mask real leaks in that file later. What stays open is the scan itself: TASK-0158's closing claim that gitleaks scans full history holds **only on the scheduled trigger**. Either set `--log-opts` so the push path scans history as the comment promises, or correct both comments to say what the push path really does — a comment that overstates a security control is worse than no comment. — ✅ 2026-11-08 (**the control was fixed, not the comment.** Reading `gitleaks-action@v2`'s own source settled it: on `push` it appends `--log-opts=-1`, on `pull_request` a commit range, and on any other event nothing — so **there is no way to make that action scan history on push**, and correcting the comment would have left `fetch-depth: 0` paid for and thrown away. Both workflows now run a **pinned gitleaks 8.30.1 binary**, its archive checksummed against the published checksums file before it executes, with no `--log-opts`, so every trigger of both workflows runs one scan over the whole history. A second defect fell out of the same reading: **the action resolved gitleaks `latest` at run time**, so the rule set moved under the repository without a commit and a scan could not be reproduced from a SHA. Also found: `security.yml` declares a `push` trigger too, so its “scans full history” comment was false on that path as well, which this entry did not record. The 2026-11-07 fixture fix is not re-broken — a full-history search for the Stripe-shaped pattern returns zero commits. **The duplication this created was removed in the same change** rather than left with a note: the scan now lives in `.github/actions/secret-scan/`, called by both workflows, exactly as TASK-0308 did for the dependency audit. **Unverified**: gitleaks could not be downloaded from this machine (`curl` exit 28 twice), so no full-history scan has actually been run here, and nothing has executed on a GitHub runner)

The job carries `timeout-minutes: 90`, so this is not the declared timeout; SIGTERM from a hosted runner at that elapsed time points at resource exhaustion (`mutation_run.py` does a `shutil.copytree` of `src/` per workspace) but the logs do not say which resource and **this is not determined**. Consequence: no survivor list has ever been produced on a schedule, so TASK-0300's findings rest entirely on manual runs with no nightly signal behind them. Reproduce locally under `/usr/bin/time -v` to find the limit it hits, bound it, and have the job emit progress unbuffered so the next SIGTERM is diagnosable from the log. — ✅ 2026-11-08 (**diagnosed and bounded; the cause is still not determined, and two of this entry's own claims were wrong.** Corrected by reading the code: the `copytree` happens **once per run**, not per workspace — `src/` 7.0 MB plus `tests/` 20 MB against ~14 GB free, so disk exhaustion is not plausible and TASK-0303's addition of `tests/` did not change that; and **the log silence is by construction, not evidence of an early crash**, because `main()` writes nothing to stdout until every mutant has run. **Leading hypothesis: memory exhaustion from a pathological mutant.** `--timeout` bounds a mutant's seconds, not its bytes, and the operators in play are exactly the class that turns a bounded loop unbounded (`remaining > 0` → `>=`, a stride `-` → `+`); a child allocating freely reaches 16 GB well inside 300 s, and a hosted VM answers that by killing the agent, which surfaces as a shutdown signal rather than a step failure. The two kills are at **different** elapsed times across trees whose mutant sets differ, which fits ‘a particular mutant does it’ and not a deterministic step fault. Workflow side now: `PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1`, `--budget 40` → `10`, `timeout-minutes` 90 → 45, `/usr/bin/time -v` when present, and **a resource sampler running concurrently inside the step** printing `MemAvailable`, `SwapFree`, free disk and the largest resident process every 10 s — concurrent rather than an `if: failure()` post-mortem, because no post-mortem step runs after a runner shutdown. If memory is the cause, the next failure will say so in the live log. The runner changes that would convert this from a kill into a recorded verdict are TASK-0321)
