The wire format is unit- and socket-tested (TASK-0151 ✅) but no real Langfuse has accepted a batch, so the field names are verified against the documented API rather than against the server — ✅ 2026-08-09 (**executed as TASK-0236**, which ran it against a real self-hosted Langfuse 3.225.1: the batch was accepted, the trace came back under fasterRag's own id with all four stage observations and the generation's 386+51 token usage, and the exercise found a real defect — a 207 with rejected events was being reported as a successful export. Ticked here by the 2026-08-09 claims audit, which found this entry still open and still asserting "no real Langfuse has accepted a batch" after that had stopped being true)
- [x] TASK-0152: `GENERATION_PRICES_USD_PER_MILLION_TOKENS` covers only `gpt-4o-mini` and `gpt-4o`. Every other generation model contributes nothing to `fasterrag_cost_usd_total`, so the cost panel silently understates spend rather than reporting an unknown. The table needs the remaining configured providers, and the panel needs a way to say "some traffic is unpriced". (The metric itself is now verified live: a real query recording 386 prompt and 51 completion tokens on `gpt-4o-mini` published `8.85e-05`, matching `386×0.15/1e6 + 51×0.60/1e6` exactly. Superseded in scope by TASK-0168.) — ✅ 2026-08-09 (**Priced**, each rate read from the vendor's own pricing page on 2026-08-09 and recorded with that source and date on the entry itself: *OpenAI generation* — 33 chat/reasoning models (`gpt-5.6-sol/-terra/-luna`, `gpt-5.5`/`-pro`, `gpt-5.4`/`-mini`/`-nano`/`-pro`, `gpt-5.2`/`-pro`, `gpt-5.1`, `gpt-5`/`-mini`/`-nano`/`-pro`, `gpt-4.1`/`-mini`/`-nano`, `gpt-4o`/`-2024-05-13`/`-mini`, `o1`/`-pro`, `o3`/`-pro`/`-mini`, `o4-mini`, `gpt-4-turbo-2024-04-09`, `gpt-4-0613`, `gpt-3.5-turbo`/`-0125`/`-1106`) from developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing, which also re-confirmed the three OpenAI embedding rates already in the table; *Anthropic* — 14 ids (`claude-fable-5`, `claude-mythos-5`, `claude-opus-5`, `claude-opus-4-8/-4-7/-4-6/-4-5` + dated form, `claude-sonnet-5/-4-6/-4-5` + dated form, `claude-haiku-4-5` + dated form) from platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/overview, with `claude-sonnet-5` recorded at its standard $3/$15 and a note that the introductory $2/$10 runs to 2026-08-31, since over-estimating is the safer error; *Cohere* — `command-r-plus-08-2024` alone, the one per-token rate cohere.com/pricing still quotes verbatim; *Ollama and HuggingFace* — zero for every model, now stated as a deliberate recorded price rather than an absence, so local traffic is neither a cost nor an unpriced gap. **Left unpriced on purpose**: Cohere's current Command A / R7B lineup, whose pricing page publishes instance-hour Model Vault rates — converting those to a per-token rate needs a throughput measurement nobody has taken, and an unmeasured number is a defect by policy; `openai_compatible` gateways, which set their own prices; OpenAI's completion-only models (`davinci-002`, `babbage-002`, `gpt-3.5-turbo-instruct`), unreachable through the adapter's chat-completions call; and Cohere's three embed rates, which the pricing page no longer states per token — those keep their 2026-07-30 date rather than being re-dated to a check that did not happen. **Restructured**, because the old shape could not hold this: entries are `TokenPrice` records carrying provider, source, and check date individually. One global `prices_dated` is only ever as true as its oldest row, so re-checking one vendor silently re-dated every stale row beside it; `PRICES_DATED` survives as the derived minimum and can no longer claim a freshness no entry has. Matching now requires **provider as well as model** — the old table billed an OpenAI-compatible gateway serving `gpt-4o` at OpenAI's rate, a fabricated figure it produced silently. Renames: `PRICES_USD_PER_MILLION_TOKENS` → `EMBEDDING_PRICES`, `GENERATION_PRICES_USD_PER_MILLION_TOKENS` → `GENERATION_PRICES`, `PRICES_SOURCE` removed. 16 tests in `test_cost_metric.py`, four of them driving a real query through `GenerationService` to pin the metric behaviour: a priced model adds exactly its list price to `fasterrag_cost_usd_total`, an unknown model and a known model on an unknown gateway each add their full token count to `fasterrag_unpriced_tokens_total` and nothing to the cost, and a local model adds to neither. Panel wording and per-model coverage documented in config-reference.md, observability.md, and integrations.md. The panel's "some traffic is unpriced" half was TASK-0168.)
- [x] TASK-0153: Filed on a mis-read: a query was reported as hanging past ten minutes. It does not. Measured on the same deployment, a single query returns `http=200` in **10.78 s**; the ten minutes were my own shell budget — an ingest, a 25 s sleep, and three curls each capped at `-m 120` — not one unbounded call. `reliability.timeouts.llm_ms` is applied by the adapter at construction (`adapters/llm/base.py`), and the openai adapter passes it to its client. No defect; entry kept rather than deleted so the ledger records the correction — ✅ 2026-08-02

### S14 — Observability dashboard (last; after Langfuse proves the trace pipeline)

- [x] TASK-0045: Implement read-only self-hosted dashboard — a **separate ASGI application on its own port**, never mounted into the control-plane API. Two structural reasons: it declares no write route at all, so "observability-only" is a property of the application rather than a promise about restraint; and it shows prompts, responses, and corpus text, so an operator must be able to bind it to an internal interface while the API faces callers. Reads the metrics registry through a new public `Registry.series` accessor so the page and a Prometheus scrape cannot disagree. Every interpolated value is escaped — a trace carries user text and model output straight onto an operator's page. **Verified live over a real socket**: `GET / -> 200` showing a stored query, its tenant, and a recorded metric; `POST / -> 405`. 11 tests, including the no-write-route assertion and an XSS attempt — ✅ 2026-08-04
- [x] TASK-0184: Tenant-scope the dashboard — it now mounts the **same `AuthMiddleware` the API uses** rather than a second implementation, so it inherits authentication, scopes, and tenant matching in one place; a separate auth path would be a second thing to get wrong, and one would eventually lag the other. With `security.auth` off it stays open, which is the single-operator deployment it was built for. With tenancy on, the middleware refuses any request that does not name a tenant, and both the page and its JSON twin filter traces to that tenant — scoping the page alone would have left the leak one URL away. Its routes require `admin`, the same scope `/v1/traces` and `/metrics` need, because it reads the same data. 16 tests — ✅ 2026-08-04

### Cross-slice (scheduled opportunistically after their dependencies)

- [ ] TASK-0049: Implement remaining vector DB adapters — **Chroma landed 2026-11-03; Milvus 2026-11-07; Weaviate 2026-11-08; Pinecone remains open.** pgvector landed earlier. The Chroma adapter passes the shared `VectorDBContract` suite against a real embedded Chroma 1.5.9 store — 33 contract cases plus 17 Chroma-specific ones, 50 passed, no server and no Docker, which is why it was chosen next. Chroma has no alias, no working local snapshot/fork and no corpus-rarity scoring primitive, so the adapter supplies all three: aliases are records in a `fasterrag-aliases` collection inside the same persistent directory (proved by a *second interpreter* reading the alias back, not merely a reopened client — the mutation that scoped aliases per-process is caught only by that test), snapshots are copies into a second collection, and BM25 IDF is computed in-process. **That keyword leg reads every posting a query's terms match into the process**: correct, and not scalable the way Qdrant's server-side sparse index is — stated in integrations.md rather than hidden. Not exercised: Chroma in server/HTTP mode, concurrent writers on one directory, any corpus large enough to show the keyword leg's cost, and any chromadb version but 1.5.9. `chromadb` went into the `dev` extra on the `redis`/`psycopg` precedent so the suite cannot silently skip in CI, at the cost of ~1400 lock lines and a heavier dev install. 13 of 13 mutations killed **Weaviate landed 2026-11-08** — the fifth backend, and the first whose vendor has a competing answer to the contract's question: its native BM25, hybrid fusion and tokenizer are all **deliberately unused**, for three independent reasons any one of which is sufficient. The adapter is handed an encoded `SparseVector` and never the query text, so `query.bm25(query=...)` has no string to take; Weaviate's `word` tokenizer and stopword list would hit different terms than every other backend, which is exactly the split ADR-0007 exists to prevent; and its inverted index has **no read path for per-document term weights**, so a collection scored by it would be structurally unexportable — the defect TASK-0291 fixed everywhere else. The tempting middle road was rejected on evidence rather than taste: storing stemmed tokens in a `TEXT` property and tuning the collection's own `bm25_k1`/`bm25_b` **cannot work, because `encode_document` stores saturated term frequencies and saturation is not invertible** — the raw count a native index needs is unrecoverable, and Weaviate would normalise by real average document length where we normalise by a fixed 256. Postings are therefore two array properties with IDF computed in-adapter over the live corpus, which is Chroma's limit exactly: every document carrying a query term is read into the process, and **candidate paging is deliberately unbounded because a truncated candidate set returns a wrong top-k silently**. All 16 contract methods are supported and none is faked — aliases are records and snapshots are collection copies, neither depending on a server version or a server-side module, since an adapter whose `snapshot()` fails on every deployment that did not set `ENABLE_MODULES` is worse than one that always works. Two things are refused loudly with typed errors: a filter on a payload key living in the JSON remainder (pushing it down would match nothing and look like “no results”) and a keyword search against a collection with no postings. **No mypy override was needed** — unlike pymilvus, `weaviate-client` ships `py.typed`. **Never run against a server**: no Weaviate and no Docker daemon, so all 54 integration cases skip; 124 unit cases run against a fake that **evaluates the adapter's real `Filter` objects rather than recording them**, and raises on any shape it does not understand, so a wrongly built filter fails instead of passing by being ignored. **The highest-risk assumption**: that `ContainsAny` over an `INT_ARRAY` selects exactly the documents whose posting list holds one of those terms — document frequency is counted from that same candidate set, so an under-matching filter does not merely lose recall, it inflates IDF for every survivor and returns a confidently wrong ranking with no error anywhere. `weaviate-client` carries no advisories and the blocking audit stays at `No known vulnerabilities found, 5 ignored`, but **it caps `grpcio<1.80`**, which forked grpcio in the lock and **downgraded protobuf 7.36.2 → 6.33.6 globally** for pymilvus, qdrant-client, chromadb and the OTel gRPC exporter, which all share them.
- [x] TASK-0050: Ship Docker deployment artifacts per deployment.md (compose profiles, sizing presets) — ✅ 2026-08-06 (`Dockerfile` — two-stage, non-root uid 10001, healthchecking `/readyz` rather than `/healthz` because the latter answers before the vector database is reachable and a load balancer would send traffic too early. `docker-compose.yml` with the `core`, `qdrant`, and `dashboard` profiles, plus `deploy/compose.{small,medium,large}.yml` mapping to the sizing table. **Langfuse and Grafana are deliberately excluded**: both are already provisioned by their config toggle, and restating them would create a second source of truth that drifts the first time a pinned version changes on one side. Published ports bind to `127.0.0.1` — the API serves unauthenticated until `security.auth: true` and the dashboard renders prompts verbatim, so publishing on every interface would expose the corpus at startup. **A fabrication of mine was caught and fixed rather than shipped**: the presets referenced a `FASTERRAG_SET` environment variable that did not exist and would have silently done nothing, so it was implemented for real — the env form of `--set`, applied beneath explicit flags and validated identically. **Verified**: all four compose files pass `docker compose config`, each profile resolves to exactly the documented services, and the presets' limits and overrides appear in the resolved output. **The `Dockerfile` has never been built** — that needs a daemon, blocker B7)
- [x] TASK-0190: Build and run the `Dockerfile`, and bring the `core`/`qdrant` profiles up for real — ✅ 2026-08-09 (**the artifacts were broken. Five defects, none of which `docker compose config` can see, which is the only thing that had ever been run against them.** (1) **The image could not be built at all, ever.** The builder stage copies `pyproject.toml`, `README.md`, `src/`, and `config.yaml`, but the wheel target force-includes `.env.example` as well (TASK-0170 ✅), so hatchling aborted with `Forced include not found: /build/.env.example` during metadata generation — before one dependency resolved. `.dockerignore` already re-includes the file past its own `.env.*` rule, so only the `COPY` was missing. (2) **`/readyz` could never have passed.** `config.yaml` ships `vector_db.host: localhost`, right on a host and wrong in a container, and nothing overrode it: measured, the api answered `503 dependencies are not ready: vector_db` on every probe and the worker exited `VECTOR_DB_UNAVAILABLE` into a restart loop. Fixed with `FASTERRAG_SET: vector_db.host=qdrant` in the base file, repeated in all three sizing presets — compose merges `environment` per key, so a preset that sets `FASTERRAG_SET` replaces the base value outright instead of extending it, and the presets would otherwise have re-broken what the base file fixed. (3) **The worker reported itself unhealthy forever.** A container inherits its image's `HEALTHCHECK` whatever command it runs, and the image probes `:8000`, which a worker never binds; the compose comment claiming the worker has "no healthcheck" described an intention, not the file. Now `healthcheck: disable: true`. (4) **The `dashboard` profile started no dashboard.** It ran `serve --port 8080`, which moves the *API* onto 8080 — the dashboard only starts when `observability.dashboard` is on, so the profile published a second copy of the API under the dashboard's name, with an inherited healthcheck pointing at a port nothing bound. Now sets `observability.dashboard=true` and leaves the API on 8000, which makes the inherited healthcheck correct rather than needing an override. (5) **`deploy/compose.large.yml`'s `ports: []` drops nothing.** Compose merges sequences by appending, so the base file's `127.0.0.1:8000:8000` survives into the rendered config; with `replicas: 3` that is two api containers that cannot bind their host port. The comment justified the empty sequence as the standard-YAML alternative to `!reset` — it parses cleanly and does nothing, which is the worse failure. Replaced with `ports: !reset null` and that one file excluded from the `check-yaml` hook, since `yaml.safe_load` rejects the Compose-only tag. **Measured** — Docker 29.6.2 / Desktop 4.85.0, Windows 11 + WSL2, compose project `fasterrag-build-probe` on shifted host ports so it could not touch the running dev fleet: `docker build -t fasterrag:local .` takes **178 s** cold (`--no-cache`, base image already local) for a **501 MB** image (no extras, `python:3.12-slim`, Python 3.12.13 — the `ARG PYTHON_VERSION=3.12` pin builds and runs fine even though the host now develops on 3.14); `docker run --rm fasterrag:local --help` prints the CLI help and exits 0; `id` reports `uid=10001(fasterrag) gid=10001(fasterrag)` and `/app/.fasterrag` is writable both as a baked image directory and through the mounted named volume; `core` + `qdrant` + `dashboard` all reach `healthy`, `/readyz` returns `200 {"status":"ready"}` with `vector_db` "reachable in 8.862 ms" from inside the container and through the published port, the worker reaches `worker ready: pools=cpu,embed,index`, and the dashboard serves `200` on 8080; under `deploy/compose.small.yml` the limits land for real as `2 CPU / 2 GB` on the api and `4 GB` on the worker, with `FASTERRAG_SET` merged as `vector_db.host=qdrant,app.workers=2`. Torn down with `down -v`. **Two things stay unmeasured**: the stack was exercised on the no-extras image, because `docker compose up` reuses an existing `fasterrag:local` rather than rebuilding, so compose's default `EXTRAS: huggingface` only adds `sentence-transformers` on top of what was verified and `/readyz` does not touch it — and that build itself was attempted and **abandoned after 20 minutes** without finishing its dependency download, which is the same multi-gigabyte first-run cost TASK-0174 ✅ measured from the other side. An operator's very first `docker compose up` pays it)
- [x] TASK-0226: A Qdrant connectivity failure still reports `EMBED_PROVIDER_ERROR` — ✅ 2026-08-09 (every backend failure now translates to `RETRIEVAL_FAILED`, which is what `docs/api-reference.md` has always promised for a vector-DB outage — **the doc was right and the code was wrong**. The two render different problem documents, so a stopped Qdrant reported as 'Embedding provider failed' sent an operator to inspect a healthy embedding provider while their vector database was down, and made a real embedding outage indistinguishable from this one. Verified against a genuinely unreachable instance: `code=RETRIEVAL_FAILED retryable=True`. Auth failures keep their own non-retryable `VECTOR_DB_AUTH_FAILED`, since a rejected key fails identically forever and naming the outage would be the wrong answer in a confident tone. Salvaged from the TASK-0225 agent, which was terminated by a session limit mid-fix)
- [ ] TASK-0087: Publish the fasterrag package to PyPI (wheel, extras, hash-locked deps, SBOM) at the first tagged beta. Distribution mechanics are now verified: `python -m build` produces both artifacts, `twine check` passes on both, and the wheel installs into a clean venv and runs. **Remaining blockers before a first publish** — TASK-0164 (license decision; the only irreversible one, since a PyPI version number can never be reused) and TASK-0020 (beta version stamp). The supply chain landed with TASK-0158 ✅; only the SBOM remains here, where it attaches to a tagged release
- [x] TASK-0170: `pip install fasterrag` was unusable out of the box — every command needs a `config.yaml`, and the missing-config error told the user to "copy the canonical config.yaml from the repository root", which an installed package does not have. Added `fasterrag config init`: it writes the canonical config plus `.env.example`, force-includes both into the wheel so the template cannot drift from the documented one, refuses to overwrite without `--force`, and never writes `.env` itself. Verified against a built wheel in a clean venv from an empty directory: `doctor` → names the command → `config init` → `config validate` passes — ✅ 2026-08-02

### Audit follow-ups (first formal audit, 2026-08-02)

- [x] TASK-0156: Fail fast on accepted-but-unenforced config — `security.auth`, `security.multi_tenancy`, and both `cost.*_token_budget` keys validate today and are consumed by nothing; enabling any of them must raise `ConfigError` naming the missing slice (mirror the implemented `cache.backend: redis` pattern) until enforcement lands with TASK-0046 — `_reject_unenforced_settings` runs in `load_settings` before the env-var check, lists every enabled key at once so an operator learns all of them in one restart, and leaves `0` (the documented "unlimited") accepted; the canonical config is asserted to still start — ✅ 2026-08-02
- [x] TASK-0157: Enforce the coverage gate in CI — `--cov-fail-under=85` scoped to `core/`, `adapters/`, `workers/`; the 87% measured today is met by discipline, not by a gate — added as its own CI step after the unrestricted run. Scoped deliberately: a repository-wide threshold is diluted by CLI plumbing and API wiring, so it can stay green while the retrieval and adapter code it exists to protect regresses. Re-measured before setting it at 87% branch coverage over 3525 statements — ✅ 2026-08-02
- [x] TASK-0158: Supply chain per security.md §6 — `uv.lock` committed (135 packages, 1577 sha256 hashes), `uv lock --check` gates CI and was verified to exit 1 on drift and 0 when clean, gitleaks scans full history, and the dependency audit is now **blocking** rather than advisory. The audit runs against the lockfile export rather than an installed environment — those differ, and only the lockfile describes what every machine installs — and measured clean across all 135 packages. The SBOM stays with TASK-0087, where it attaches to a tagged release — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [ ] TASK-0159: Implement the `cache_only` degradation rung — consult the semantic cache when retrieval raises, serving `degraded: true, mode: cache_only` instead of a bare `RETRIEVAL_FAILED`; pairs with the breaker (TASK-0148) and the scope decision (TASK-0165)
- [x] TASK-0160: Add a Windows CI leg running the fast suite — the project is developed on Windows and TASK-0141's path-case defect is exactly the class ubuntu-only CI cannot catch — added as a `windows-latest` job running the fast suite only; lint, format, and typecheck are not repeated because they are platform-independent and already gate on the quality job. Two shipped defects were Windows-only: the path-case document id and the UTF-8 BOM the config loader rejected — ✅ 2026-08-02
- [x] TASK-0161: Add a nightly scheduled CI job running the eval and benchmark suites with ledger-format artifacts, so the provable-claims policy is infrastructure rather than habit — `.github/workflows/nightly.yml` runs the eval and benchmark suites at 03:00 and uploads ledger-format artifacts with 30-day retention. The workflow states in its own header that its numbers are **not citable**: a shared GitHub runner has neighbours, and performance.md requires an isolated documented machine. It is a regression signal; TASK-0084 still needs real hardware — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0162: Add a CI check diffing the FastAPI-generated OpenAPI schema against api-reference.md's endpoint table, catching endpoint drift automatically (the `traces list` drift was caught by hand) — `scripts/check_openapi_drift.py`, wired into the quality job. It found seven real discrepancies on its first run: `GET /v1/traces` and `POST /v1/retrieve` served but undocumented, and five documented endpoints nothing serves. All seven are now resolved — the two are documented, and the five carry an explicit **Not yet implemented** marker that the gate honours, so a reader sees the gap rather than the script merely tolerating it. It also catches the reverse drift: an endpoint that got built while the marker was left behind — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0163: Implement the `FasterRag` facade per the python-api.md status table — `from_config`, `from_settings`, async context manager, `ingest`, `query`, `query_stream`, `retrieve`, `estimate`, `index_lock`. A composition layer only: it builds the same services `api/dependencies.py` builds, so no behavior can exist here that the REST API lacks, and a test asserts no pipeline logic crept in. Exported lazily through `__getattr__` — `import fasterrag` stays at 6 ms while resolving the facade costs 4.4 s. Verified end to end against live Qdrant and OpenAI: estimate 3 docs/189 tokens, ingest 3 indexed, retrieve 3 hits, query answered with a citation, stream 59 events `meta`→`done`, lockfile present — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0171: `FasterRag.collections`, `.doctor`, `.replay`, and `.export_archive`/`.import_archive` are still unimplemented on the facade; the CLI and REST surfaces cover them today. The python-api.md table marks each one — `doctor`, `collections`, `create_collection`, `drop_collection`, and `replay` on both facades. `create_collection` takes the dimension from the embedding router rather than an argument, because a collection whose width disagrees with the model writing into it fails on the first upsert. `replay` runs with no trace store and no cache, so investigating an incident cannot alter the evidence. `doctor` works unstarted — it exists to diagnose installations that cannot start. Archives remain unwrapped because TASK-0079 has not built them — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0172: `fasterrag.sync` blocking facade — the documented wrapper over the async one, for callers not running an event loop. Owns its event loop (created on `__enter__`, closed in a `finally` so a shutdown failure cannot leak it), refuses to start inside a running loop rather than deadlocking, and keeps `query_stream` incremental by pulling one event at a time — measured first token at 0.91 s over a 51-event response. Verified live: retrieve 3 hits, query answered in 4.30 s with a citation, stream `meta`→`done` — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0174: The shipped `config.yaml` defaults to `retrieval.rerank: true` with `BAAI/bge-reranker-v2-m3`, a **2.2 GB** cross-encoder. A fresh `pip install` user's first query therefore downloads 2.2 GB and then loads it — measured as **not completing within 400 s** on a developer laptop, presenting as a hang with no output. Two things to decide: whether the out-of-box default should be a small reranker (or `rerank: false`) with the large model as an opt-in, and whether model loading should emit progress so a first run looks slow rather than broken. Found while verifying the sync facade, where it masqueraded as a facade bug — the *silent* half is fixed: the first-load message is now a **warning**, not an info line, because nothing configures logging in a plain script and Python's last-resort handler only emits warnings. It names the cost and the two ways out (`retrieval.rerank: false`, or a smaller `retrieval.reranker_model`), and the load duration is logged on completion. The **default** half is deliberately left open as TASK-0175: whether to ship a smaller reranker out of the box is a retrieval-quality decision for the maintainer, not a bug fix — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0176: `normalise_source` (TASK-0141) changed the document id of every path-based source, so collections indexed before it no longer match ids computed after it — observed as `handbook_baseline` scoring a flat 0.0 until re-ingested. Added `IDENTITY_VERSION`, recorded in `index.lock` and compared by `detect_drift`, so the mismatch is *reported* by `index lock verify` rather than presenting as a broken retriever. A lockfile written before the field existed defaults to scheme 1, never to the current value: defaulting to "current" would declare a stale index up to date and hide the very mismatch the field was added to surface. 7 tests — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0177: Filed on a broken diagnostic, not a defect. The parser is correct: `parse_bytes` on the affected file yields codepoint `U+2014`, an in-process and a spawned-subprocess `parse_and_chunk` both preserve it, an adapter round-trip returns the string byte-identical, and re-reading the stored payload with an explicit UTF-8 decode shows `em_dash=True mojibake=False` on all 12 documents. The mojibake was in my check: piping curl into `json.load(sys.stdin)` decodes with the Windows console codepage rather than UTF-8, so correct UTF-8 bytes were mangled on the way in. Entry kept rather than deleted so the ledger records the correction — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [ ] TASK-0175: (maintainer decision) The shipped default is `retrieval.rerank: true` with `BAAI/bge-reranker-v2-m3` (2.2 GB). **Figure corrected 2026-11-04 by BENCH-0003**: the old “over 400 s to load” conflated download with load — the weights download in 357 s and the model then loads in **8.3–9.3 s** from a populated cache, about a second more than a 90 MB model, so the first-run pain is a one-time fetch and not a per-process cost. The recurring cost is larger and was not previously quantified: **17–25×** the small model's per-query rerank latency, which at the shipped `retrieval.rerank_top_n: 100` is **55.7–86.9 s per query** CPU-only. Restate the trade against these numbers before deciding. Reranking is expected to be the strongest quality lever in the stack — expected, not measured (TASK-0084) — so turning it off by default trades an unquantified quality gain for a faster first run. The alternatives are a smaller default cross-encoder, or keeping it and treating the first-run cost as documented. TASK-0174 made the wait visible; this decides whether it should exist
- [x] TASK-0173: Entry-point plugin groups (`fasterrag.vectordb` / `.embeddings` / `.llm`); the factories resolve built-ins only. Split out of TASK-0163, which shipped the facade half — the premise was stale: all three factories already resolved entry points. Verified rather than assumed, with 15 tests registering a fake plugin against each group — resolution, origin reporting, rejection of a non-adapter, and the rule that a built-in name always wins so an installed package cannot silently take over a configured provider — ✅ 2026-08-03
- [x] TASK-0166: Add a CI self-truth check that fails when CLAUDE.md or README claims a documentation-only state while `src/` exists — the audit found exactly that inversion — `scripts/check_doc_truth.py`, wired into CI. Deliberately narrow: it asserts only the cheap-to-verify, expensive-to-get-wrong facts and ignores fenced code blocks, because a doc gate that fires on judgement calls gets disabled. Parameterised by root so the gate itself is tested — eight tests, including a reconstruction of the audit's actual inversion. Left out of pre-commit at the maintainer's direction; CI-only — ✅ 2026-08-02
- [x] TASK-0167: Run `fasterrag doctor --json` as a CI smoke job against the CI environment itself (dogfoods D10 on every push) — added to the quality job, and it exercises the packaged-template path with it: `config init` in an empty directory, then `doctor --json`. Placeholder keys are set so doctor gets past config validation and actually runs its environment checks; without them it would exit 4 on the first one and pass trivially. Exit 4 is accepted afterwards (CI has no Docker daemon, and reporting that is doctor working); a crash still fails the job — ✅ 2026-08-02
- [x] TASK-0168: Turn the generation price table into dated data with an unpriced-traffic counter metric so the cost panel can say "some traffic unpriced" instead of silently understating (extends TASK-0152) — added `fasterrag_unpriced_tokens_total{provider,model}`. An unpriced model previously contributed nothing to the cost total, which made the dashboard understate spend while looking healthy; the panel could not say "some traffic is unpriced" because nothing counted it. The cost panel's description now states that unpriced traffic is excluded and that a non-zero counter means the figure is low — ✅ 2026-08-03

### Claims audit follow-ups (second provable-claims audit, 2026-08-09)

- [x] TASK-0238: Execute the periodic provable-claims audit CLAUDE.md mandates — ✅ 2026-08-09 (**scope**: all 33 files under `docs/` including `adr/`, plus `README.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, and every docstring, module header, and `# CRITICAL:` block under `src/` — roughly 4,800 lines of prose and ~230 source modules. **Method**: five sweeps, each run rather than eyeballed. (1) A superlative regex over every doc (`fastest`, `most efficient/optimized/reliable`, `best-in-class`, `unmatched`, `industry-leading`, `state-of-the-art`, `highest`, `biggest`, `single largest`) — 16 hits, of which 8 were the provable-claims policy quoting itself and 3 were explicit goals ("aims to be", "its goal is to be"), both compliant; **8 were unbacked claims and all 8 were corrected**. (2) A verification-language sweep (`verified`, `confirmed`, `measured`, `benchmarked`, `proven`) over docs and `src/` — ~60 hits, most legitimately tracing to a ticked ledger entry; **9 were rewritten** to say that the check was a one-off manual run rather than a committed regression test, which is what they actually were. (3) A provenance sweep for bare numbers. (4) A **scripted** dangling-id check comparing every `TASK-`/`AUDIT-`/`BENCH-` reference in the tree against the ids this ledger defines. (5) A declaration-versus-implementation check of every capability the docs describe as shipped or unshipped, against `src/`.
  **Headline finding — one fabricated number had propagated to nine files.** "Reranking adds ~100–300 ms per query" and its companion superlative "the single biggest quality lever" appeared as plain fact in `architecture.md`, `scope.md`, `glossary.md` (which pins *canonical* definitions), `config-reference.md`, `flow.md`, `troubleshooting.md` (twice), `integrations.md`, `ADR-0004`, and `blockers.md`, and `performance.md` cited it circularly as "the documented 100–300 ms band". **It traces to nothing**: no ledger entry isolates the rerank stage, no `references.md` row carries a rerank latency, and the ledger's only two entries are both marked non-citable. It could not be verified cheaply either — the default cross-encoder is 2.2 GB and TASK-0174 ✅ measured it at over 400 s to load. **Withdrawn everywhere**, with each site now saying the stage is unmeasured and pointing at TASK-0084.
  **Second finding — stale negative annotations, in both directions.** The 2026-08-02 audit annotated docs "specified-not-built"; the slices that then built those things (TASK-0045/0046/0132/0148/0150/0151/0173/0179/0189/0214/0232) did not remove the markers. **Nineteen capability statements were wrong**: the observability dashboard, API auth, multi-tenancy, the circuit breaker, OTLP trace *and* metric export, contextual enrichment, entry-point plugins, the pgvector adapter, Langfuse/Grafana startup triggering and doctor gating, and the D11 archive were all documented as unbuilt while shipping. The most dangerous was `cookbook.md` R5, which told operators that flipping `security.auth: true` "changes nothing, and the API serves unauthenticated" — advice that would have led someone to leave a *working* control switched off. Corrected, and `failure-modes.md`'s as-built note, which asserted the circuit breaker was unimplemented, was inverted back.
  **Third finding — capabilities documented as shipped that do not exist.** `cache_only` (the middle degradation rung) appears nowhere in `src/` yet was described as built in six places, including the chaos-suite table D12 cites as its proof. The runtime cost governor does not exist: `BUDGET_EXCEEDED` is defined and raised by nothing, `estimated_cost_usd` is in no response, and **three cookbook recipes shipped configs that raise `ConfigError` at load** — R9, the recipe aimed at first-time evaluators, also named `chroma`, an adapter that is refused by name. The load, soak, and mutation test layers are documented as required parts of the pyramid and **do not exist** (no k6/Locust script, no soak suite, `mutmut` not a dependency; the `load`/`soak`/`mutation` markers are declared and used by zero tests) — four FMEA rows name one of them as their proving test. `performance.md` claimed nightly runs "append to the ledger" and file regressions "automatically"; the nightly workflow uploads artifacts and does neither.
  **Dangling ids**: 6 found, 2 of them deliberate test fixtures (`BENCH-0007`, `TASK-9999`) and left alone; **4 were real** — `TASK-0221`/`TASK-0222` cited in `pgvector.py` and `TASK-0225` in `qdrant.py` pointed at ids this ledger has never held. The qdrant one is a *true* claim wearing a wrong id (the measurement is TASK-0226 ✅) and was repointed; the pgvector two named real pending work with no task behind it, now filed as TASK-0239/TASK-0240.
  **Also corrected**: `README.md` principle 5 still told contributors to use feature branches, contradicting the trunk-based rule CLAUDE.md has carried since 2026-07-30; `reliability.md` carried TASK-0153 as an open timeout violation when that task was closed as a mis-read with no defect; `security.md` §6 still claimed non-root for *every* provisioned image, the exact wording AUDIT-0007 ✅ narrowed in `deployment.md` and never propagated; `migration-guide.md` claimed "we ship six vector DBs" (two) and that every number in the docs is TBD-marked (at the time, at least seven were not).
  **Also found while rebasing**: TASK-0178 was still open and still asserting "no real Langfuse has accepted a batch" after TASK-0236 ✅ had done exactly that work earlier the same day — ticked here, and `blockers.md`'s B7 heading repointed at TASK-0079, the id that genuinely still blocks that group.
  **Totals: 91 edits across 30 files** — 3 source files (docstrings and one `# CRITICAL:` block only; **no behaviour was touched**, and the unit suite reports the same 2029 passed / 6 skipped before and after) and 27 documents. **Nine claims could not be resolved and are filed as open tasks below** (TASK-0239 through TASK-0247). **Two judgement calls recorded rather than acted on**: "the fastest way to see what a deployment customised" in `cli-reference.md`, `cli/commands/diagnostics.py`, and `api/query.py` is idiomatic English about which command is quickest to type, not a performance claim about the software, and was left alone; and `disaster-recovery.md`'s 4,311 ms restore figure was left because the document already refuses to publish it as an RTO and states its scope, which is the behaviour the policy wants. `CHANGELOG.md` was given a standing provenance banner rather than having its dated historical observations rewritten)
- [x] TASK-0239: Pool connections in the pgvector adapter — ✅ 2026-08-11 (it held one connection and serialized every operation on it, which its own module docstring admitted. In an async, multi-worker system that makes the adapter a global lock on the whole vector-DB path: one blocked statement stalls every other caller, including a `health()` that runs `SELECT 1` and needs no lock at all. Now a `psycopg_pool.AsyncConnectionPool` sized by `vector_db.pgvector.pool_max_size` (default 8, per process). **Proved by construction rather than by timing**, which matters because a stopwatch on a laptop running Docker measures the laptop: an external session takes an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock on the collection's table, so an adapter statement touching it blocks for exactly as long as the test chooses — no sleeps and no races. Mutation-verified against a live PostgreSQL 17 + pgvector: forcing the pool back to size 1 fails the concurrency case. No speedup is quoted anywhere, because this hardware fails benchmark-ledger rule 5)

- [x] TASK-0240: Give the pgvector adapter a statement timeout that distinguishes queries from index builds — ✅ 2026-08-11 (only the connect was time-bounded, so a slow query or a runaway index build could hang indefinitely. Ordinary statements now carry `reliability.timeouts.vector_db_ms` as a session `statement_timeout`; schema changes, snapshot copies, and restores carry `vector_db.pgvector.maintenance_timeout_ms` (default 600s) applied with `SET LOCAL`, so the longer budget cannot leak back to the pool on the connection that used it. **The split is the whole point and is what the test pins**: a bound tight enough for a query aborts the HNSW build on any collection large enough to need one, so giving maintenance the query budget fails the case where a blocked snapshot must outlive it — mutation-verified exactly that way. The longer budget is still a bound, not an absence of one, pinned by a second case where a blocked snapshot is cancelled at it. Both settings live under `vector_db.pgvector` rather than `reliability` because a connection pool and the `statement_timeout` GUC are PostgreSQL concepts no other adapter can honour, and a shared key only pgvector reads would be silently ignored by every Qdrant deployment)

- [x] TASK-0241: Write the chaos and reindex scenarios the docs name but the suite does not contain — ✅ 2026-11-02 (config-mutation and network-partition landed 2026-10-25; the parked reindex-under-continuous-query-load suite is now salvaged and landed as `tests/chaos/test_reindex_under_load.py`, all 8 cases, nothing skipped or weakened. **The reported '2 of 8 time out under the full suite' did not reproduce** — 23 standalone runs, 3 chaos-directory runs and 5 full-suite runs were green before anything changed, including 12 runs with the box pinned by 10 CPU burners. The honest statement is that the original failure was not observed, not that it was diagnosed. What *was* found and measured is the mechanism that produces exactly that symptom: the suite paced its rebuild by spinning on `asyncio.sleep(0)` inside a 30-second `wait_for`, so a query loop that stopped producing observations for any reason cost the full budget and surfaced a bare `TimeoutError` with the real cause buried underneath — an injected dead loop took **30,000.3 ms** to report on the parked version and **0.2 ms** on the landed one. The fix is an event handshake, not a longer timeout; the backstop stays 30 s and is now documented as a deadlock backstop rather than a budget. Nothing in `src/` is implicated: `services/reindex.py`, `services/querying.py` and `core/breaker.py` hold no lock, so no deadlock between the rebuild and the load generator is reachable. One real bound is pinned rather than fixed — a hybrid query issues its dense and sparse legs as two searches that resolve the alias independently, so a swap landing between them returns one result fused across both generations, which bounds D2's guarantee to a single retrieval leg; asserted as a measured fact with a `# TODO:` at the seam rather than quietly fixed from a test ticket. 5 consecutive chaos runs green (43 passed, 10 skipped), full suite 2538 passed)
- [x] TASK-0242: Implement the D9 runtime cost governor — ✅ 2026-08-11 (built rather than narrowed: `services/budget.py` enforces both budgets before the provider is called, and `BUDGET_EXCEEDED` is now raised by something. **Budgets are checked against the worst case, not the likely one** — the prompt's real size plus `llm.max_tokens`, because a completion's length is unknown until the provider has produced it and by then the money is spent; a cap that admits a request it cannot pay for is not a cap. The tenant is then charged what the query *actually* cost, at the single point both the streaming and non-streaming paths pass through: admitting on the ceiling and charging the actual is the pair that neither overspends nor over-refuses. **The placement of the check is the subtle part.** It sits *outside* the try block that implements the degradation ladder, because that block absorbs retryable failures into an extractive answer — a retryable budget error would have turned a spend control into a quality regression nobody could see. `BUDGET_EXCEEDED` is already non-retryable in the problem table, so it would re-raise regardless; keeping the call outside means that stays true even if the ladder's conditions are ever loosened. **A caveat is documented rather than hidden**: the per-tenant counter is in-process, exactly like the per-key rate limiter (TASK-0216), so N replicas grant N times the budget — and for a spend cap that error runs in the permissive direction. It ships anyway because a single-replica deployment gets precisely what it configured and because refusing to start taught operators nothing, but security.md, cookbook.md and config-reference.md all now say so outright. The rolling window is fixed at 24h and documented, since "rolling" without a period means nothing. The empty `_reject_unenforced_settings` guard was retired rather than kept as a no-op with an unused parameter, its two refusal tests converted to acceptance tests following this file's own precedent for auth and tenancy, and the rule it embodied moved into the loader's module docstring. 11 governor tests plus 5 end-to-end; seven mutations, all caught — **one only after being rewritten**: the ceiling check survived its first test because a budget below the prompt size refuses either way and cannot tell prompt-only from prompt-plus-ceiling apart, so the budget now sits deliberately between the two)

- [ ] TASK-0243: Build the three testing-pyramid layers that are documented as required and do not exist — **load** (k6/Locust, hardware-annotated), **24 h soak** (no memory/fd growth), and **mutation testing** (mutmut on chunk-boundary and retrieval-scoring logic). The `load`, `soak`, and `mutation` markers are declared in `pyproject.toml` and carried by zero tests, and four FMEA rows cite one of these suites as the test that proves them. Mutation checking is currently done by hand per slice, which is the practice this would automate — **mutation layer landed 2026-11-04; load and soak remain open and still need the reference hardware TASK-0084 waits on.** `scripts/mutation_run.py` is purpose-built on CPython's `ast`, chosen on evidence: mutmut 3.8.0 installs and then refuses to run on Windows ('please use the WSL'), and cosmic-ray 8.7.0's parso raises a **false SyntaxError on valid code in this tree** (the parenthesized `with` in `services/archive.py`) while allowing one global test-command per session. **Nothing under `src/` is ever written** — the tree is copied per run and pytest pointed at the copy, proven by injecting a raise into the copy and watching the suite go red only with the override. Measured on four of six targets, exhaustively: **134 mutants, 71 killed, 63 survived, 183 s**; `retrieval` (52) and `chunking` (234) were not measured and no score is claimed for them or for the tree. Wired into `nightly.yml`, never the push path: **a survivor warns and the job stays green** — the set contains provable equivalent mutants and one result that changed between two runs of the same code, reported rather than smoothed — while **exit 2 fails**, because a target whose suite was already red kills every mutant for free and would report a perfect score (T12), verified live. Survivors go to the job summary, not only an artifact. 20 self-mutations, 19 red; the one green is a provable equivalent mutant, and two guard breaks green on the first pass were fixed with new tests before re-verifying
- [x] TASK-0244: Wire the D7 retrieval regression gate into CI — **prerequisite found 2026-08-28: the committed baseline has drifted and the gate would report *blocked*, not pass.** `services/regression.py` and `fasterrag benchmark --suite eval` implement the tolerance comparison, the CLI already exits with the documented code 5 on regression, and `testing-strategy.md` listed it among the blocking gates while §6 of the same document admitted it was not wired — that contradiction is now corrected. Wiring it is a thin CI step, but it cannot pass yet: `Baseline.comparable_to` requires both the embedding model *and* `retrieval_config_hash` to match, and the handbook baseline recorded 2026-08-02 hashes to `17c2897…` against today's `7447af1…`. **Why it drifted**: the hash covers the *entire* chunking and retrieval models, so every tunable added since invalidates it — `chunking.token_counter`, `chars_per_token`, `semantic_percentile`, `retrieval.bm25_k1` and `bm25_b` were all added after the recording. That is defensible for `index.lock` drift detection, which the same hash serves, but for eval comparability it means adding a knob whose default preserves behaviour retires every baseline. **And the drift is not only cosmetic**: `token_counter` defaults to `auto`, which counts with the model's own tokenizer and therefore moves chunk boundaries, so the recorded metrics may genuinely no longer hold. Nothing caught it because `test_the_baseline_is_committed_and_loads_through_the_gate` asserts only that `config_hash` is truthy — true of any stale hash. **Order of work**: re-record the baseline against a live backend (needs Docker, which is why this is not done here), invert the drift-pinning test added alongside this entry, then add the CI step; and consider whether eval comparability wants a narrower hash than index drift does — ✅ 2026-11-03 (wired as the `eval-gate` CI job, and the prerequisite drift is fixed by **splitting eval comparability away from the `index.lock` hash**. `core/identity.retrieval_config_hash` is untouched and stays deliberately broad, because for drift detection any difference really does mean the index was built differently. `services/regression.py` now owns a separate enumerated `COMPARABILITY_FIELDS` fingerprint recorded **as values rather than a digest**, so a refusal names the field that moved instead of saying only 'different'; a field a baseline does not mention reads as that field's current default, which is what lets a behaviour-preserving addition leave a baseline alive, and `COMPARABILITY_VERSION` retires every older baseline when an addition is *not* behaviour-preserving. **Erred broad on purpose** — fields that only bite under another setting's value are included anyway, because wrong-narrow silently compares incomparable results while wrong-broad merely retires good baselines visibly; an exhaustiveness test derived from the live pydantic models fails the build if a new setting lands in neither the compared nor the explicitly-neutral list. **The numbers were NOT re-measured** — no Docker here, so the harness could not run end to end and nothing was invented. What was recovered is the configuration behind the 2026-08-02 numbers, found by hash preimage search over candidate configurations, and written in as a *derived* fingerprint: `top_k=5`, `rerank=false`, `token_counter` necessarily `estimate`. That search also showed the **ticket's stated cause was incomplete** — the drift had three causes, not one: the five added knobs, the identity-version-3 digest change on 2026-09-13 which alone retired every recorded hash, and the baselines having been recorded under a real behaviour difference no fingerprint design can wish away. The job pins exactly that configuration, so it does not cover the reranker and does not score the `policies` dataset. Its first run on a runner is the first time those numbers meet current code; a `regressed` verdict there is a finding, not a reason to loosen the tolerance. 7 mutations including both directions of the default-reading rule, all red)

- [x] TASK-0245: Consult the `llm` circuit breaker — ✅ 2026-08-10 (the breaker existed and nothing read it, so reliability.md's "one breaker per provider" overstated the protection and FMEA row 20's "retries → breaker opens → extractive" was only ever retries. Verified worse than filed: `create_breakers` was reachable **from tests alone** — no production code called it, so the `llm` series never reached the `fasterrag_circuit_state` gauge at all, and an absent series reads as healthy on every dashboard. `GenerationService` now owns a breaker for its whole life (rebuilding one per query would count to one forever and never open), checks it *inside* the existing try so an open circuit takes the same route a live outage takes — `CircuitOpenError` is retryable by construction, making load-shedding a rung on the ladder rather than a 503 — and records failure and success on both the `complete` and `stream` paths. The dead `create_breakers` factory was removed rather than wired: its stated purpose, publishing `closed` from startup, is already met by `CircuitBreaker.__post_init__`, confirmed by constructing a service and watching the `llm` series appear on the gauge. Four tests, all three hooks mutation-verified — the first version of the success test was vacuous (one failure below threshold leaves the breaker closed whether or not the count is cleared) and now runs fail → succeed → fail against a threshold of two, so only clearing keeps it shut)

- [x] TASK-0246: Make nightly regression detection real, or stop describing it — ✅ 2026-11-02 (built the comparison half, dropped the bug-filing half, and `performance.md` §3 now describes only what happens. `scripts/compare_nightly.py` compares two `fasterrag benchmark --json` documents — warmed-median p50/p95/p99 and throughput, per suite — against a 10% tolerance, reusing `services/regression.py`'s rules so improvements never fail and an incomparable baseline is refused rather than waved through. **Three outcomes with distinct exit codes**: 0 within tolerance, 1 regressed, 2 not comparable — so a night that compared nothing can never read as green, and anything left unchecked downgrades the whole night. `nightly.yml` finds the previous run through the Actions API and pulls its artifact with `download-artifact@v4` (`run-id` + `github-token`, under `permissions: actions: read`), failing the job only on a real regression. Auto-filing was dropped **on purpose**: todo.md is the only task file, and these shared-runner numbers are explicitly not citable, so a machine filing entries from them would build a second task list out of measurements the methodology forbids citing. 27 unit tests against 25 mutations, all killed — two of which were vacuous until M24/M25 were added, and one only became real once it asserted on the *reason* rather than the verdict. **Unverified**: nobody has watched the previous-run lookup or the cross-run download execute on a real runner; the first scheduled nightly is its first evidence, and performance.md §3.7 carries that caveat)
- [x] TASK-0247: Cite the R3 chunk-size preprint properly or withdraw the numbers it backs. The `references.md` row names no title, no authors, and no link, so neither the "~2,500-token context cliff" nor the "~512–1024-token working range" can be checked by a reader or a reviewer — ✅ 2026-10-25 (**the source was found and the numbers were withdrawn anyway, which is the whole point.** The paper is real — arXiv:2601.14123v1, Bennani & Moslonka, 20 Jan 2026 — and genuinely contains the phrase 'context cliff' at ~2.5k tokens, so filling the row in and declaring both claims evidence-backed would have satisfied the task on its face. Reading the experimental grid instead showed the 2,500 figure is indexed on **context budget C, not chunk size S**, and that the 512–1024 range falls entirely outside the swept range of S (50–500, step 50). Citing a real paper for a number it does not state is worse than citing nothing, because the citation makes the claim look checked. So R3 is now cited in full *and* records what it does not support; the chunk-size range is withdrawn and `chunking.chunk_size: 768` is documented as a configurable default with a stated rationale rather than a published optimum; and the cliff is redefined across the tree as a bound on **total retrieved context**, not a chunk-size ceiling — including in `glossary.md`, which is the canonical-terminology file and so was the highest-leverage wrong definition in the repository. Seven documents plus `config/loader.py` carried the old claims and were swept. The verifier independently re-fetched the paper and confirmed every figure. Two tensions surfaced by importing a real source and are recorded rather than quietly resolved: R3 measures no benefit from overlap while `chunking.overlap` ships at 64, and the assembled context budget ships at 4,000 tokens against a cliff the repo now tells readers sits at ~2,500 — the second is filed as TASK-0285)

### Release-readiness review follow-ups (2026-08-10)

- [x] TASK-0251: Serve Langfuse and Grafana provisioning over REST — ✅ 2026-08-11 (reproduced on `main` first: `api/admin.py` held `_PROVISIONABLE = frozenset({"qdrant"})` while `docs/api-reference.md` line 226 documented `qdrant`, `langfuse`, *or* `grafana` as supported, so `POST /v1/admin/provision/langfuse` answered NOT_FOUND against a doc that promised it. Neither an endpoint test nor the OpenAPI drift gate could see it — the route existed and was served, and the disagreement lived in a *value inside it*, which is why the release-readiness review found it by hand and the automated gates did not. Fixed structurally rather than by widening the set: `services/provision_registry.py` is now the one table of what can be provisioned and the three verbs each tool answers, and the CLI's `choices=`, the REST surface, and the error message listing what is supported all read it — there is nothing left to keep in sync. Salvaged from a worker stopped mid-verification, and it was **incomplete in a way that mattered**: it had written the `DELETE /v1/admin/provision/{tool}` row into api-reference.md and a test exercising the `down` verb, but never added the route, so the doc described an endpoint that answered 405 — documented-before-built, which CLAUDE.md forbids. The CLI has `--down` for all three tools, so parity genuinely required it and it was implemented rather than the doc narrowed. It stops containers only: data volumes and generated secrets survive, because regenerating Langfuse's `SALT`/`ENCRYPTION_KEY` would invalidate every credential issued against them. Not tenant-scoped — a container is one process-wide resource, so scoping would imply a per-tenant stack that does not exist — and guarded by the `admin` scope the middleware applies to the whole `/v1/admin` prefix. Mutation-verified: reverting the registry to qdrant-only fails 6 tests, removing the DELETE route fails 7. OpenAPI drift gate green)

- [x] TASK-0255: Gate `docs/internals.md`'s spec→module map against the real tree, the way `tests/unit/docs/test_reference_parity.py` gates the CLI and config references — a module named in the map that no longer exists, or a new top-level module absent from the map, should fail the docs suite — ✅ 2026-10-25 (`tests/unit/docs/test_internals_parity.py`: 24 rows parsed, 86 implementation paths resolved against `src/fasterrag/` and 40 test paths against `tests/`, both directions asserted. The parser had to learn two idioms the reference gate did not need: a bare filename is written relative to a directory the same cell named *earlier but not necessarily last* — the observability row names `core/tracing.py` and then returns to `observability/` — so every prefix seen in the cell is a candidate; and `fixed/recursive/semantic/layout/late.py` is shorthand for five siblings, expanded only after the literal path fails and only when every member resolves. **The first version of the headline test was vacuous and the adversarial reviewer caught it**: `test_every_top_level_package_has_a_row` matched a substring against the whole document rather than the parsed table, so with every table row stripped it stayed green while the three parse guards went red. It now asserts against resolved row tokens, and the strip experiment was re-run to watch it go red. The second direction the ledger asked for — a new top-level *module* absent from the map — was also missing, because the walker filtered to directories with `__init__.py`; eight root modules were invisible to it. Five of those are documented re-export shims and sit in a named exemption set that a further test keeps honest in both directions, rather than being given filler rows. Exhaustiveness is asserted only where the document actually promises it, and the docstring says what is and is not covered. **The gate's real yield was six false facts in internals.md**, the first document a rebuilder reads: the D9 row said the runtime cost governor 'does not exist' when `services/budget.py` had been shipped and wired for ~2.5 months; `_reject_unenforced_settings` was cited in three places and exists nowhere; and the LLM-breaker, disk-full-typing, pgvector-pooling and REST-parity rows all still described shipped work as open. Caught by eye while writing the parser, not by the parser — path parity cannot catch a false prose sentence whose paths all resolve, which is this gate's honest limit)
- [ ] TASK-0256: Delete the pre-release banner in `docs/how-to-use.md` and the "not yet published" rows in `docs/python-api.md`/README as part of release step 3 in `docs/release.md` — filed now so the release checklist cannot forget the docs that promise to update themselves
- [x] TASK-0252: Reconcile the coverage gate — ✅ 2026-08-12 (the true state of the three was the third: the gate's *scope* drifted, though not its configuration. Measured at **83.13%** against the 85 threshold, and the shortfall was one file — `adapters/vectordb/pgvector.py` held **338 of the ~680 missing statements and branches, half the total**, four times the next worst. The cause was an asymmetry between the two reference adapters: `test_qdrant.py` carries 38 unit tests against a fake client while pgvector had **no unit test file at all** — 1400 lines covered by 17 integration tests, and the coverage run deselects integration by design. A 1400-line adapter joined the gated packages carrying nothing the gate could see, which is the whole 87% → 83% fall. Fixed the way testing-strategy.md §2 asks — by restoring coverage rather than lowering the bar, and without excluding pgvector from the scope, since it is exactly the adapter code the gate exists to protect. 41 unit tests now cover identifier derivation, the SQLSTATE-to-taxonomy matrix, filter translation, and the pooled operations behind a fake pool: pgvector went from roughly 20% to **44%**, and the gated packages from 83.13% to **85.59%**. **The rounded display would have hidden this**: an intermediate run showed `TOTAL ... 85%` while `--cov-fail-under` reported 84.54% and failed, so the precise figure is what was checked throughout. Writing the tests turned up one real defect — `_dsn()` documented itself as refusing an "unset or empty" variable but tested `if not dsn:`, which passes a whitespace-only value straight through to psycopg; the config loader has treated blank as missing all along, and the adapter now matches it. Headroom is 0.59 points, thinner than the 87.11% the gate was set against: the remaining gap is the CRUD surface — upsert, search, update, delete — which needs richer fakes than the read paths did, and is filed as TASK-0265)
- [x] TASK-0265: Cover the pgvector write path in unit tests — ✅ 2026-08-28 (the guards that run *before* a row is written turned out to be the valuable part, and all three are static methods needing no fake at all: a dimension mismatch names both widths and the offending point rather than the batch, because the cause is a changed embedding model and the fix is a reindex, not a retry; a sparse vector aimed at a collection with no keyword index is refused, since accepting it would write the dense half and silently discard the leg the caller asked for, leaving a collection that answers hybrid queries with half an answer; and an unknown collection is `NOT_FOUND` rather than an empty result that writes nowhere. Also covered: the empty-batch early return, which touches no pool at all, and the terms-table derivation an operator needs to pair two tables in a database. pgvector went from **44% to 51%** — from roughly 20% before TASK-0252 — and the gated packages from 85.59% to **86.31%**, so headroom against the 85 threshold is now 1.31 points rather than 0.59, close to the 87.11% the gate was originally set against. What is still uncovered is the SQL-emitting body of `upsert`, `search`, `update` and `restore_snapshot`, which the integration suite owns against a real server and which a fake can only assert the shape of; that is a deliberate stopping point rather than an omission)
